Sample complexity and weak limits of nonsmooth multimarginal Schrödinger system with application to optimal transport barycenter
Pengtao Li, Xiaohui Chen

Abstract
Multimarginal optimal transport (MOT) has emerged as a useful framework for many applied problems. However, compared to the well-studied classical two-marginal optimal transport theory, analysis of MOT is far more challenging and remains much less developed. In this paper, we study the statistical estimation and inference problems for the entropic MOT (EMOT), whose optimal solution is characterized by the multimarginal Schrödinger system. Assuming only boundedness of the cost function, we derive sharp sample complexity for estimating several key quantities pertaining to EMOT (cost functional and Schrödinger coupling) from point clouds that are randomly sampled from the input marginal distributions. Moreover, with substantially weaker smoothness assumption on the cost function than the existing literature, we derive distributional limits and bootstrap validity of various key EMOT objects. As an application, we propose the multimarginal Schrödinger barycenter as a new and natural way to regularize the exact Wasserstein barycenter and demonstrate its statistical optimality.

1
Introduction

Multimarginal optimal transport (MOT) problems arise naturally in a broad range of scientific domains, including economics
[
23
,
29
]
, finance
[
10
,
40
]
,
machine learning
[
18
,
77
,
4
]
, tomography
[
1
]
, quantum physics
[
16
,
33
]
, and the study of Wasserstein barycenters
[
2
]
. Given
m
Borel probability measures
\nu_{j}
on
bounded subsets
\mathcal{X}_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
j\in[m]:=\{1,\dots,m\}
and a real-valued cost function
c:\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{m}\to\mathbb{R}
, the MOT seeks to solve the following optimization problem

\displaystyle\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{m}}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})d\pi(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}),

(1)

where
\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
denotes the set of all possible couplings with marginal distributions
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
. The special case of
m=2
coincides with the classical theory of optimal transport (OT)
[
90
,
91
,
81
]
. Recent years have witnessed substantial advances in the computational and statistical aspects of OT
[
76
,
28
]
, owing in part to the Monge structure and regularity properties of the optimal coupling in the two-marginal setting
[
13
,
17
,
42
]
.

In contrast, the general scenario of MOT with
m\geq 3
marginals is far less-understood than the two-marginal OT setting beyond the existence of the optimal coupling as a solution of (
1
), and many theoretical questions currently remain largely open
[
73
]
. One fundamental obstacle is that, unlike the unified theory for the classical OT problem, existing results of MOT rely heavily on the special forms of the cost function
c
. The first result on the optimal coupling structure dates back to
[
43
]
for the quadratic cost function
c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\sum_{i\neq j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{2}
, with a series of follow-up work under cost functions of the same type
[
54
,
72
]
, and other types of cost functions such as repulsive cost
[
32
]
and cyclic cost

[
74
]
. More generally, as demonstrated in
[
73
]
, multimarginal twist conditions on the cost function that imply structural results on the resulting optimal coupling can be proposed but are much more restrictive than their two-marginal counterparts. All the subtlety hints the fundamental divergence from the classical case
m=2
to the multimarginal setting
m\geq 3
; cf.
[
73
,
59
,
74
,
75
]
for more details and state-of-art results. On the computational side, the MOT problem is computationally intractable to solve or even approximate with a general cost function
[
5
]
, highlighting the root difference from the two-marginal OT problems.

Many applications of MOT require costs that can capture the intrinsic structure, rather than squared Euclidean costs.
In quantum physics, electron–electron
repulsion is modeled by the Coulomb interaction cost

c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{-1}

[
64
,
69
,
38
]
.
Bounded, continuous truncations, such as

c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\min\{1,\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|^{-1}\}
,
are also studied to remove the collision singularities

[
15
]
.
In mathematical finance, the cost

c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=-\mathbf{1}_{\{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{m}\geq s\}}

arises in robust risk aggregation, where one seeks the worst-case
probability that aggregate losses exceed a given threshold, subject to
prescribed marginal distributions

[
39
]
.
A geometrically different example is

c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=-|\det(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})|
, with

x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, which corresponds to maximizing the
expected volume of the simplex with vertices
0,x_{1},\dots,x_{m}

over all couplings with the prescribed marginals

[
25
]
.

In this work, we study the statistical estimation and inference for a closely related MOT problem from data. Motivated by the prevalent entropy regularization of the OT problem
[
34
,
71
,
79
]
, we consider the following
entropic multimarginal optimal transport
(EMOT) problem with a regularization parameter
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})}\int_{\mathcal{X}}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})d\pi+\varepsilon\text{KL}(\pi\|\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k}),

(2)

where
\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k}
denotes the product measure of
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
. Since the unique solution (i.e., optimal coupling) of EMOT is characterized via a Schrödinger system of equations
[
24
,
68
]
, we also refer problem (
2
) as the
multimarginal Schrödinger system
and its optimal coupling as the
multimarginal Schrödinger coupling
. In practice, we do not directly have access to the marginal distributions
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
. Instead, one often only has a cloud of data points
X^{(j)}_{1},\dots,X^{(j)}_{N}
drawn from
\nu_{j}
for
j\in[m]
. To study statistical properties of EMOT, we work with the standard sampling model throughout this paper; that is, for each
j\in[m]
, the cloud of data points
X^{(j)}_{1},\dots,X^{(j)}_{N}\sim\nu_{j}
are i.i.d., and moreover, the
m
samples drawn from
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
are independent.

Statistical estimation and inference of EMOT from data are scarce and somewhat fractured in literature, with papers addressing cost functions with particular structures such as smoothness; see Section
1.2
for the related work.
One of the aims of this paper is to establish a new pipeline of statistical tools from sharp sample complexity to principled uncertainty quantification for general nonsmooth EMOT problems.
The difficulty for understanding and analyzing EMOT problem under nonsmooth cost function
c
is two-fold. On the one hand, as mentioned above, the multimarginal setting introduces substantially more delicate and intricate nonlinear interactions and coupling among the marginal distributions than in the classical two-marginal case. On the other hand, progress in the two-marginal OT regime and especially the
m\geq 3
regime has traditionally relied on strong structural assumptions on the cost function, with smoothness playing an almost indispensable role. Abandoning smoothness eliminates many standard techniques and makes the analysis significantly more challenging. Consequently, moving from the two-marginal case to the multimarginal setting (
m\geq 3
), or from regular smooth costs to general nonsmooth costs, each on its own introduces significant challenges that are not merely technical but are intrinsically tied to the fundamental structural complexity of the problem.

As a leading application of our general EMOT results, we consider statistical analysis of the Wasserstein barycenter, a notion of averaging for measure-valued data under the optimal transport metric
[
2
]
. Barycenters have found numerous applications and advantages in data science and machine learning
[
84
,
94
]
, computer graphics and image processing
[
78
,
83
]
and scalable Bayesian inference
[
84
]
. Despite widespread interests, computing the Wasserstein barycenter remains extremely challenging in high (or even moderate) dimensions, partially due to the curse-of-dimensionality barrier
[
7
]
. This exponential dependence in dimension
d
explains the popularity of various regularized modifications for solving problems of large scale
[
65
,
22
,
11
,
55
,
30
]
. While many existing works focus on the approximating and algorithmic issues, considerably less is known on the statistical performance for estimation and performing inference on Wasserstein barycenters when we only have random sample access to the input marginal measures.

In Section
5
, we propose a new approach, termed as the
multimarginal Schrödinger barycenter
(MSB) to regularizing the barycenter functional based on the EMOT formulation. In contrast to existing regularized barycenter methods, the MSB applies regularization directly to the MOT problem. This yields a more controlled form of regularization that preserves the fidelity of the unregularized barycenter while offering both statistical and computational benefits.

1.1
Contributions

Below we summarize our main contributions.

•

Under the standard statistical sampling model where only random point clouds are accessible to the input marginal distributions, we establish
dimension-free
and
parametric

\sqrt{N}
-rates of convergence for estimating the cost functional (Theorem
4.1
) and multimarginal Schrödinger coupling on any bounded test function (Theorem
4.3
).

•

We further derive the nonparametric rate of convergence of empirical multimarginal Schrödinger coupling on the Hölder smooth function class (Theorem
4.4
). When specializing this general result to the
p
-Wasserstein distance for
p=1,2
, we obtain a sharp convergence rate that substantially improves the state-of-the-art rates
[
9
]
. To the best of our knowledge, the current work is the first to obtain the optimal convergence rates under
W_{p}\,(p=1,2)
distance of entropy regularized couplings in the Wasserstein space given the known lower bounds on empirical Wasserstein distances
[
41
,
93
]
.

•

We prove the central limit theorem (CLT) for the quantities of interest, including the cost functional (Theorem
4.5
) and optimal Schrödinger coupling on any bounded test function (Theorem
4.6
), and demonstrate the associated bootstrap consistency (Theorem
4.9
and Theorem
4.10
), where the latter enables downstream data-driven statistical inferences.

•

For the MSB, we also derive parallel rates of convergence, weak limits and bootstrap consistency (Corollary
5.4
, Theorem
5.5
, Corollary
5.7
, Corollary
5.8
), as an application of general multimarginal Schrödinger system theory established for EMOT.

•

From a technical standpoint, we design a new decoupling mechanism (see Appendix
C
) to handle multimarginal Schrödinger system based on induction and the iterative use of its marginal feasibility. This new probability tool, inspired by the idea of higher-order degenerate
U
-statistics
[
27
]
, can be of independent interest to other MOT and barycentric inference problems. Building on this, along with other new technical tools, we show in Lemma
4.8
that under the assumption that the cost function
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
, optimal population potential
\bm{f}^{*}
and optimal empirical potential
\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}
satisfy that

\displaystyle\|\bm{f}^{*}-\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{N}}\right),

achieving a genuine improvement over the previously known results
[
37
,
21
]
.

The following Table
1
summarizes our main results and the corresponding regularity assumptions on the cost function
c
.

1.2
Related work

Because of the aforementioned challenges, EMOT under nonsmooth cost has not been well-investigated in the exisinting literature. The well-posedness (existence, uniqueness and smooth
dependence with respect to the data) for the multimarginal Schrödinger
system was first established in
[
24
]
for bounded cost function
c
. For smooth cost function,
[
21
]
obtained the Lipschitz continuity of the map from marginal distributions to the optimal Schrödinger coupling.
[
50
]
investigated the weak limits of unbalanced multimarginal optimal transport under smooth cost function but could not specify the limiting variance. The Hadamard differentiability for EMOT was established in
[
44
]
for smooth cost function. All the results above considered the empirical plug-in estimation of EMOT system while
[
87
]
employed neural networks and obtained parametric estimation rate for the cost functional with special graph structure.

The Wasserstein barycenter for probability measures on the Euclidean domain was first introduced in
[
2
]
and later extended to Riemannian manifolds in
[
60
]
, where existence and uniqueness were proved when at least one of the input measures vanishes on small sets. The extension to the setting
of nonsmooth extended metric measure spaces was recently done by
[
52
]
. Statistical consistency for empirical Wasserstein barycenters on a general geodesic space was derived in
[
62
]
, and rates of convergence were established under additional curvature assumptions
[
3
,
49
]
. Practical algorithms for computing unregularized Wasserstein barycenter can be found in
[
35
,
57
,
58
,
6
]
. There are several existing regularization models for the barycenter functional
[
35
,
65
,
22
,
11
,
55
,
30
]
. However, those works lack either of thorough statistical analysis for estimation or inference of the barycenter, or of efficient algorithm to compute its barycenter. Different from our MSB proposal based on the EMOT formulation, most existing regularized barycenters begin by introducing regularization into the pairwise formulation of the barycenter problem (
20
).

1.3
Notations

For a nonempty compact set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
that agrees with the closure of its interior,
\mathcal{C}(\Omega)
denotes all the continuous functions on
\Omega
. We occasionally omit the dependence on the space
\Omega
when it could be inferred from the context. Given
(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X}_{j})
,
\bigoplus(f_{1},\dots,f_{m}):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}
. For every multi-index
k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|k|=\sum_{j=1}^{d}k_{j}
(where
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
),
define the differential operator
D^{k}
by

D^{k}f\;=\;\frac{\partial^{|k|}f}{\partial x_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{d}^{k_{d}}},\qquad\text{with }D^{0}f=f.

For every
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, denote by
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\Omega)
the set of functions
f
on
\Omega
such that
f
has
continuous derivatives of all orders
\leq s
on
\mathrm{int}(\Omega)
and these derivatives have continuous
extensions to
\Omega
(so
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\Omega)=\mathcal{C}(\Omega)
). Define the norm

\lVert f\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\Omega)}\;=\;\sum_{j=0}^{s}\;\max_{|k|=j}\;\lVert D^{k}f\rVert_{\infty,\,\mathrm{int}(\Omega)}.

Then
\big(\mathcal{C}^{s}(\Omega),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\Omega)}\big)
is a separable Banach space. For
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m})

denote the space of vector-valued functions

\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m}):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

whose coordinate functions belong to
\mathcal{C}^{s}(\Omega)
,
equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m})}=\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|f_{j}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{s}(\Omega)}.

The Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence or relative entropy between probability
P
and
Q
is defined as

\text{KL}(P\parallel Q):=\begin{cases}\int\log\left(\frac{dP}{dQ}(x)\right)dP(x),&\text{if }P\ll Q,\\
+\infty,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
P\ll Q
means that
P
is absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
and
\frac{dP}{dQ}(x)
denotes the Radon-Nikodym derivative. We write
\xi_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\xi
(resp.
\xi_{n}\xrightarrow{w}\xi
) for convergence in probability (resp. weak convergence). For a probability measure
\mu
and a measurable function
f
, we often write
\mu(f)
to represent
\int fd\mu
. We write
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\nu)}
as the essential supremum for
\|f(X)\|
with
X\sim\nu
, and
L^{\infty}(\nu)
denotes the Banach space of functions such that
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\nu)}<\infty
. Given probability measures
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
,

\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
denotes the set of all probability measures
\pi
with
\nu_{i}
as the
i
-th marginal, i.e.,
\ (e_{i})_{\sharp}\pi=\nu_{i}\ \text{where}\ e_{i}:(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\mapsto x_{i}
is the
i
-th projection map for each
i\in[m]
.

We are given
m\geq 2
probability space
(\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i},\nu_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,m
and set

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}:=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i};\quad\mathcal{F}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{F}_{i};\quad\nu:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\nu_{i}.

Given
i\in\ [m]
, we will denote by

\mathcal{X}_{-i}:=\prod_{j\neq i}\mathcal{X}_{j}
,

\nu_{-i}:=\bigotimes_{j\neq i}\nu_{j}
,
and will always identify
\mathcal{X}
with
\mathcal{X}_{i}\times\mathcal{X}_{-i}
, namely, will denote
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathcal{X}
as
x=(x_{i},x_{-i})
. The empirical distribution given
N
independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) sample of
\nu_{k}
is denoted as
\hat{\nu}_{k}^{N}
for
k\in[m]
. Similarly as before, we write for
i\in[m]
,
\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-i}:=\bigotimes_{j\neq i}\hat{\nu}^{N}_{j}
.

Throughout the rest of the paper, we work with a compact domain
\mathcal{X}_{i}\subset

\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a non-empty interior. Without loss of generality, by rescaling we may further assume that
\mathcal{X}_{i}\subset[-1,1]^{d}
,
i\in[m]
. For clarity and brevity, more definitions of the commonly used notations are provided in Appendix
A
.

1.4
Organization

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we collect background material for the EMOT problems. Optimization geometry of the dual functional of EMOT is introduced in Section
3
. The main results on sample complexity, weak limits and bootstrap validity for the empirical EMOT problem are collected in Section
4
. Statistical guarantees of MSB are established Section
5
. The Appendix contains proofs that are omitted from the main text, technical tools used in the proofs, and other auxiliary results.

2
Background and mathematical preliminaries

2.1
Background on OT

In this section, we present some background on the optimal transport theory.
Given two probability measures
\mu,\,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\Omega)
, the 2-Wasserstein distance between
\mu
and
\nu
is defined as the value of the Kantorovich problem:

W_{2}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\left\{\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\|x-y\|^{2}d\pi(x,y)\right\}^{1/2}.

(3)

Solving the linear program in (
3
) on discretized points in
\Omega
imposes tremendous computational challenges. Entropy regularized optimal transport computed via Sinkhorn’s algorithm has been widely used with an efficient approximation at guaranteed low-computational cost even for high-dimensional probability measures
[
34
]
. The entropic OT (EOT) problem is defined as

S_{\varepsilon}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\left\{\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\|x-y\|^{2}d\pi(x,y)+\varepsilon\text{KL}(\pi||\mu\otimes\nu)\right\},

(4)

where
\varepsilon>0
is the regularization parameter. It is known that problem (
4
) has a unique solution
\pi^{*}\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
and admits a strong duality form
[
67
]
, i.e., zero duality gap (up to an additive constant depending only on
\varepsilon
), given by

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)\\
g\in L^{\infty}(\nu)\end{subarray}}\left\{\int_{\Omega}fd\mu+\int_{\Omega}gd\nu-\varepsilon\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\exp{\left(\frac{f(x)+g(y)-||x-y||^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)}d(\mu\otimes\nu)(x,y)\right\}.

The above supremum is achieved at a unique pair of the optimal dual potentials

(f^{*},g^{*})\in L^{\infty}(\mu)\times L^{\infty}(\nu)
up to translation

(f^{*}+c,g^{*}-c)
for
c\in\mathbb{R}
. In addition, the optimal coupling

\pi^{*}
can be recovered from the optimal dual potential pair
(f^{*},g^{*})
via

\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi^{*}}{\mathrm{d}(\mu\otimes\nu)}(x,y)=\exp\left(\frac{f^{*}(x)+g^{*}(y)-\|x-y\|^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right).

2.2
EMOT and strong duality

In the literature, the EMOT problem (
2
) is also referred as the multimarginal Schrödinger problem because it is equivalent to minimize the relative entropy with respect to a Gibbs kernel associated with the transport cost
[
68
,
24
]
, i.e.,
\min_{\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})}\ \text{KL}(\pi||\kappa),

where
\kappa
is the Gibbs kernel

d\kappa(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\propto\exp\left(-c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})/\varepsilon\right)\;d(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k}).

Since we work in a bounded domain
\mathcal{X}\subset([-1,1]^{d})^{m}
, problem (
2
) admits a strong duality in the
L^{\infty}
setting
[
68
,
24
]
,

\sup_{f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\nu_{j})}\Big\{\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int f_{j}d\nu_{j}-\varepsilon\int\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}-c}{\varepsilon}\right)d(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})\Big\}.

(5)

Supremum in (
5
) is achieved at a unique pair of the optimal (Schrödinger) dual potentials

\mathbf{f}^{*}:=(f_{1}^{*},\dots,f_{m}^{*})\in\prod_{i=1}^{m}L^{\infty}(\nu_{i})
up to translation

(f_{1}^{*}+c_{1},\dots,f_{m}^{*}+c_{m})
for
(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfying
\sum_{j=1}^{m}c_{j}=0
. We denote by
\bm{f}^{*}
the unique optimal potential vector satisfying
\nu_{k}(f^{*}_{k})=0
for
k\in[m-1]
. In addition, the optimal (Schrödinger) coupling

\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}
can be recovered from the optimal dual potentials
\mathbf{f}^{*}
and marginal distribution
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
via

\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}}{\mathrm{d}(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j})}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}^{*}(x_{j})-c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})}{\varepsilon}\right)=:p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}).

(6)

Note that (
6
) is equivalent to the multimarginal Schrödinger system: we have
\nu_{j}
-almost surely for each
j\in[m]
,

f_{j}^{*}(x_{j})=-\varepsilon\log\left\{\int\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{i\neq j}f_{i}^{*}(x_{i})-c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})}{\varepsilon}\right)d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})\right\}.

(7)

This allows the optimal potential
\mathbf{f}^{*}
to be extended as continuous functions when the cost function is continuous, and provides a functional analytic perspective that could be beneficial for our statistical analysis. Particularly, we could equivalently consider the extension map

\displaystyle\bm{T}:\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X}_{j})

\displaystyle\to\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X}_{j})

(8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bm{T}(\mathbf{f})=\left(T_{1}(\mathbf{f}),\dots,T_{m}(\mathbf{f})\right)

defined for
j\in[m]
and
x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
as

T_{j}(\mathbf{f})(x_{j}):=\varepsilon\log\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}_{-j}}e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(x_{i})-c(x_{j},x_{-j})}{\varepsilon}}\,d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})\right).

(9)

A function
\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})
is called optimal (Schrödinger) potential associated to
\bm{\nu}:=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
if it solves the Schrödinger system

\bm{T}(\mathbf{f})=0.

(10)

As noted above, if
\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})
solves (
10
) for some fixed
\bm{\nu}
, then so does every family of potentials of the form
(f_{1}+c_{1},\dots,f_{m}+c_{m})
where the
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}=0
. This defines an equivalence relation
\sim
and we define, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the quotient space

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{k}:=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathcal{X}_{j})\right)/\sim.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
is a Banach space when endowed with the quotient norm
\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{k}}
(the infimum of the norm over all representatives in the equivalence class). We use
[\bm{f}^{*}]
to denote the equivalence class of the optimal potential
\bm{f}^{*}
.

In the following, we present some basic properties of EMOT and its optimal dual potentials and show that the the optimizer
\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon}
of EMOT in (
2
) converges to the optimal coupling
\pi^{*}_{0}
of the unregularized MOT in (
1
), whenever unique, as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Proposition 2.1
(Approximation).

Assume the cost function
c
is continuous. Any cluster point of Schrödinger couplings
(\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is an optimal coupling
\pi^{*}_{0}
of MOT problem (
1
). If futher
\pi^{*}_{0}
is unique, then we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}W_{2}(\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon},\pi^{*}_{0})=0.

Proposition 2.2
(Bounded dual potentials).

The optimal dual potential
\bm{f}^{*}
satisfies that

\max_{j\in[m]}\|f^{*}_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq 3\|c\|_{\infty}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}f^{*}_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq(2m+1)\|c\|_{\infty},

where
\|f^{*}_{j}\|_{\infty}:=\|f^{*}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\nu_{j})}
and
\|c\|_{\infty}:=\|c\|_{L^{\infty}(\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\nu_{i})}
.

3
Optimization Geometry

In this section, we recognize an appropriate optimization geometry for the EMOT problem, which will serve as a foundation for subsequent developments. Specifically, a key structure for proving sharp statistical rates of convergence and later weak limits for EMOT quantities (such as cost functional, coupling, potentials and resulting barycenter) in Sections
4
and
5
is to recognize an optimization geometry of
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f})
that respects the optimal multimarginal coupling structure in (
6
). For this purpose, we shall consider the gradient of
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f})
as elements of the dual space of
\mathscr{L}_{m}:=\left(L^{2}(\nu_{1}),\dots,L^{2}(\nu_{m})\right)
. This space could be identified with
(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X}_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X}_{m}))
or
(\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{m}))
through the extension in (
9
) depending on the regularity of the cost function
c
; however, since the distinction is immaterial for our purposes, we shall not differentiate between them.

Motivated by the geometry for two-marginal entropic OT problem
[
79
,
85
]
, we first generalize the gradient to multi-marginal setting.

Definition 3.1
(Gradient of the dual objective).

The gradient
\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}:\mathscr{L}_{m}\to\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{m}
is defined as

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}),\mathbf{g}\right\rangle_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}=\int\Big[\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{j}\Big)\Big(1-\exp\Big({\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}-c}{\varepsilon}}\Big)\Big)\Big]d\left(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k}\right),

where
\mathscr{L}^{\prime}_{m}
denotes the dual space of
\mathscr{L}_{m}
.

The optimization geometry w.r.t.
\mathscr{L}_{m}
induces a norm of the gradient. For
\mathbf{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\in\mathscr{L}_{m}
, we define
\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}:=(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int g_{j}^{2}d\nu_{j})^{1/2}.

Lemma 3.2
(Norm of dual objective gradient).

We have

\left\|\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f})\right\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int\Big[\int 1-\exp\Big({\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}-c}{\varepsilon}}\Big)d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})\Big]^{2}d\nu_{j}.

For
L\geq 0
, we define the convex subset
\mathcal{S}_{L}
of dual potentials

\mathcal{S}_{L}:=\Big\{\mathbf{f}\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}L^{\infty}(\nu_{j}):\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})}\leq L,\,\,\nu_{i}(f_{i})=0\,\,\text{for}\,\,i\in[m-1]\Big\}.

Proposition 3.3
(Strong concavity of dual objective).

The dual objective
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
is concave with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}
on
\mathscr{L}_{m}
, namely
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f})-\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{g})\geq\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}),(\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{g})\right\rangle_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}
for all
\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\in\mathscr{L}_{m}
. In addition,
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
is
\beta
-concave with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}
on
\mathcal{S}_{L}
with
\beta=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{L+\|c\|_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}\right)
, i.e., for all
\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{S}_{L}
,

\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f})-\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{g})\geq\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}),(\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{g})\right\rangle_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}+\frac{\beta}{2}\|\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}^{2}.

(11)

Throughout the rest of this paper, we assume that the regularization parameter
\varepsilon>0
, number of marginals
m
and cost function
c
are
fixed
.

4
Main Results

This section summarizes the main results of the paper. Section
4.1
establishes rate of convergence of several EMOT related objects (cost functional, Schrödinger coupling) under different discrepancies. Section
4.2
derives the weak limits of these quantities while Section
4.3
proves the bootstrap consistency.

Let
\hat{\nu}_{j}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{(j)}_{k}}
be the empirical distribution of
\nu_{j}
. The empirical EMOT problem corresponding to (
2
) is defined as

S_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m}):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m})}\int_{\mathcal{X}}cd\pi+\varepsilon\text{KL}(\pi||\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m}),

(12)

and the empirical dual objective functional is defined as

\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int f_{j}d\hat{\nu}^{N}_{j}-\varepsilon\int\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}-c}{\varepsilon}\right)d(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\hat{\nu}^{N}_{k}).

(13)

As in the population case (cf. Section
2.2
), we denote
\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}=(\hat{f}^{*}_{1},\dots,\hat{f}^{*}_{m})
as the unique (up to translation) maximizer of
\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f})
,
with
\hat{\nu}^{N}_{k}(\hat{f}^{*}_{k})=0
for
k\in[m-1]
.
To lighten the notation, we denote
\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}):=\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{k}(x_{k})-c(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})}{\varepsilon}\right)
. Similar as the Schrödinger map extension in the population version (
9
), the marginal feasibility constraints enable us to do the following canonical extension: for
x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j},\,j\in[m]
,

\hat{f}_{j}^{*}(x_{j})=-\varepsilon\log\left\{\int\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{i\neq j}\hat{f}_{i}^{*}(x_{i})-c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})}{\varepsilon}\right)d\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-j}(x_{-j})\right\}.

From this point on, will employ this extended empirical optimal potential and use
[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]
to denote the equivalence class of the optimal potential
\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}
. The empirical dual objective function also enjoys similar bounded potential property and strong concavity w.r.t. to the geometry
\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{m}:=\left(L^{2}(\hat{\nu}_{1}^{N}),\dots,L^{2}(\hat{\nu}_{m}^{N})\right)
; see Appendix
D
for precise formulations.

4.1
Sample complexity of quantities of interest

Our first main result is the convergence rate under the mean squared error (MSE) for the empirical cost to its population cost. The proof is deferred to Appendix
E
.

Theorem 4.1
(Cost functional).

For a given bounded cost function
c:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
C=C(m,\varepsilon,\|c\|_{\infty})>0
depending only on
m,\varepsilon,\|c\|_{\infty}
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[S_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m})-S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})\right]^{2}\leq\frac{C}{N}.

(14)

We highlight that the sample complexity result in Theorem
4.1
does not require any smoothness assumption on the cost function
c
, as long as it is bounded on the domain
\mathcal{X}
. Moreover, one may choose
C_{m,\varepsilon,\|c\|_{\infty}}=m^{3}\varepsilon^{2}e^{(10m+10)\|c\|_{\infty}/\varepsilon}
(up to a numeric constant) in (
14
). Dependence on
\varepsilon
is well-known to be exponentially poor even for
m=2
without further leveraging the additional smoothness of the cost function, and existing statistical convergence results do not allow
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
; see, for example,
[
8
,
9
,
79
]
.

Remark 4.2
(Comparison with existing sample complexity bounds).

First, our result (
14
) is
dimension-free
and stronger than Theorem 5.3 in
[
9
]
where an intrinsic-dimension dependent sample complexity bound on the cost functional was obtained under regularity conditions on the marginal distributions. More specifically, they showed that: for
d_{\nu}>2s
,

\mathbb{E}\left|S_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m})-S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})\right|\lesssim_{m,\varepsilon,\|c\|_{\infty}}N^{-s/d_{\nu}},

(15)

where
d_{\nu}
is an intrinsic dimension parameter of marginal distributions and
s
is the order of differentiability of the entropy regularized MOT problem. Clearly, our convergence rate is much faster than (
15
) which suffers from curse-of-dimensionality, and we do not need any regularity assumption on the marginals
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
and the smoothness of the cost function
c
. In addition, we mention that the constant in Theorem 5.3 for entropy regularization case in (
15
) is also exponentially poor with respect to
\varepsilon
. Next, for smooth cost with special graphical representation structure, Proposition 2 in
[
87
]
indicated a parametric rate for a neural network estimator when the neural network width
w=O(N)
. In contrast, our Theorem
4.1
demonstrates that simple plug-in sample estimator for the EMOT can achieve the optimal rate of convergence with minimal regularity and smoothness.

Our next task is to present a dimension-free concentration bound for the empirical Schrödinger coupling to their population analogs when acting on any bounded test function. The following Theorem
4.3
not only proves such a parametric rate guarantee, but also serves as a stepping stone for deriving sharp rates of convergence under more general discrepancy measures (including
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
distances) between
\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon}
(cf. Theorem
4.4
below).

Theorem 4.3
(Schrödinger coupling).

Suppose that
\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon}
) is the Schrödinger coupling of Problem (
2
) (resp. Problem (
12
)) with bounded cost function
c:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then there exists a constant
C:=C(m,\varepsilon,\|c\|_{\infty})>0
such that for any
g\in L^{\infty}(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j})
, and for all
t>0
, we have with probability at least
1-(2m^{2}-2m+2)e^{-t}
,

|(\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}-\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon})(g)|\leq C\|g\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}.

(16)

Proof of Theorem
4.3
can be found in Appendix
D
. Here, we want to emphasize that the crux to establish Theorem
4.3
is embedded in Lemma
C.1
on a novel concentration bound for the empirical gradient norm. Theorem
4.3
specialized to two marginal case was first obtained by
[
79
]
. Lemma
C.1
marks the major technical divergence from the two-marginal case
m=2
in
[
79
]
, and its challenge deserves particular clarification.

When the number of marginals
m\geq 3
, there are substantial obstacles in proving the concentration of the empirical gradient norm of dual objective functional (Lemma
C.1
). The argument in
[
79
]
is based on the standard Hoeffding inequality by simply stacking the independent samples, which
only
works for two marginals. For
m\geq 3
, the nonlinear dependency structure in the empirical gradient creates highly nontrivial statistical challenges. Specifically, one needs to
simultaneously
handle all marginal constraints of the multimarginal Schrödinger system by fully harnessing the independence among samples from different marginals. In words, there is no such issue in the 2-marginal case and simple stacking works. Thus, our technique and results are
not
extensions of
[
79
]
from two- to
m
-marginal, since their results heavily rely on the specific structure of two-marginal Schrödinger system. To resolve the intricate dependencies inside the multimarginal system, one has to find the appropriate approach to decouple its structure in order to exploit the characteristics of the system. Our key Lemma
C.1
(concentration of empirical gradient norm) and its supporting induction argument for decoupling are highly nontrivial and require new probability tools inspired from high-order
U
-statistics
[
27
]
to capture the nonlinearity in the dual objective gradient tailored for
m\geq 3
.

Next, we can quantify the convergence rate of Schrödinger coupling over a rich class of test functions. Specifically, we consider the standard
\beta
-smooth Hölder function class
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}:=\mathcal{H}([-1,1]^{md};\beta,L)
, where
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{X};\beta,L)
contains the set of functions
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\|f\|_{\beta}:=\max_{|k|\leq\lfloor\beta\rfloor}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|D^{k}f(x)|+\max_{|k|=\lfloor\beta\rfloor}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\neq y\\
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\end{subarray}}\frac{|D^{k}f(x)-D^{k}f(y)|}{\|x-y\|^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}}\leq L

for some parameter
L>0
and
\lfloor\beta\rfloor
being the largest integer that is strictly smaller than
\beta
. Note that
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{X};1,L)
is the class of Lipschitz continuous functions on
\mathcal{X}
with constant
L
.

Theorem 4.4
(Schrödinger coupling convergence rate on Hölder class).

There exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
m,\varepsilon,\beta,\|c\|_{\infty}
and
d
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{g\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}|(\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}-\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon})(g)|\right]\leq\begin{cases}CLN^{-1/2}&\text{if}\quad d<2\beta,\\
CLN^{-1/2}\log{N}&\text{if}\quad d=2\beta,\\
CLN^{-\beta/d}&\text{if}\quad d>2\beta.\end{cases}

(17)

In particular, we have the following expectation bound on the
p
-Wasserstein distances for
p=1,2
:

\mathbb{E}[W_{p}^{p}(\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*},\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon})]\leq\begin{cases}CN^{-1/2}&\text{if}\quad d<2p,\\
CN^{-1/2}\log{N}&\text{if}\quad d=2p,\\
CN^{-p/d}&\text{if}\quad d>2p,\end{cases}

(18)

for some constant
C>0
depending only on
m,\varepsilon,d,\|c\|_{\infty}
.

The proof of Theorem
4.4
is deferred to Appendix
D
. When specializing this general result to the
p
-Wasserstein distance for
p=1,2
, we obtain a sharp convergence rate for entropic multimarginal Schrödinger coupling that substantially improves the state-of-the-art rates
[
9
]
. More discussions on the optimality in are presented after Theorem
5.5
in Section
5
.

It is now worth re-iterating that for all sample complexity results in Section
4.1
, we only assume that the cost function
c
is bounded, without imposing any smoothness conditions. This advantage enables us to approximate a broader class of aggregation notions of probability measures, even going beyond the Wasserstein barycenter in Section
5
. For instance, one could approximate Wasserstein median with entropy regularization by the multimarginal formulation of Wasserstein median, cf. Theorem 5.1 in
[
20
]
. However, we do not further pursue the direction of statistical analysis for nonsmooth robust functionals in this paper and leave it as a future work.

4.2
Weak limits of quantities of interest

Now, we consider the weak limits of several EMOT related objects. We start with the CLT for the cost functional whose proof is deferred to Appendix
E
.

Theorem 4.5
(CLT for cost functional).

For a given bounded cost function
c:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\sqrt{N}\left(S_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m})-S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})\right)\overset{w}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}\right),

as
N\to\infty
, provided
\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\text{Var}_{X_{j}\sim\nu_{j}}(f^{*}_{j}(X_{j}))>0
.

To the best of our knowledge, Theorem
4.5
provides the first CLT for the EMOT cost functional under general and nonsmooth
c
. Previous statistical results have focused exclusively on the two-marginal EOT setting. Using quite different techniques, relying on the smoothness of the cost function
c
,
[
37
]
obtained the CLT for two marginal entropic OT cost functional, by carefully controlling the derivatives of every order for the optimal potential
\bm{f}^{*}
. More recently, under significantly weaker (non-smooth) assumptions,

[
48
]
got an analogous CLT for the two-marginal EOT problem.

Next, we provide the weak limit of the expectation of a bounded test function with respect to the Schrödinger coupling. Let

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{K}(y^{(j)}_{\beta},\beta=i_{j,1},\dots,i_{j,m-1};j=1,\dots,m)\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\\
=\frac{1}{\left((m-1)!\right)^{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{j}\in S_{m-1}\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\Psi(y^{(j)}_{\beta},\beta=i_{j,\pi_{j}(1)},\dots,i_{j,\pi_{j}(m-1)};j=1,\dots,m)\\
+\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{t=1}^{m-1}\left[(\nu_{-j})(g\,p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(y_{i_{j,t}}^{(j)})-(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})(g\,p^{*}_{\varepsilon})\right],\end{gathered}

(19)

where
S_{m-1}
denotes all the permutation on
\{1,\dots,m-1\}
and

\displaystyle\Psi(y^{(j)}_{\alpha},\alpha=i_{j,1},\dots,i_{j,m-1};j=1,\dots,m)

\displaystyle:=-\mathop{\text{$\int$}}\nolimits\bigoplus\left(\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},y^{(2)}_{i_{2,1}},\dots,y^{(m)}_{i_{m,1}})-1\\
p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(y^{(1)}_{i_{1,1}},x_{2},\dots,y^{(m)}_{i_{m,2}})-1\\
\vdots\\
p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(y^{(1)}_{i_{1,m-2}},y^{(2)}_{i_{2,m-2}},\dots,x_{m-1},y^{(m)}_{i_{m,m-1}})-1\\
p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(y^{(1)}_{i_{1,m-1}},y^{(2)}_{i_{2,m-1}},\dots,y^{(m-1)}_{i_{m-1,m-1}},x_{m})-1\end{pmatrix}\right)g(\bm{x})p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x})d(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})(\bm{x}).

Here,
\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}
is the inverse of (linear, bounded) operator

\displaystyle\bm{\Gamma}:\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{1}

\displaystyle\;\longrightarrow\;\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{1},

\displaystyle\bm{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{m})

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;(\bm{\Gamma}_{1}\bm{h},\ldots,\bm{\Gamma}_{m}\bm{h}).

with
\bm{\Gamma}_{k}\bm{h}=h_{k}+\sum_{l\neq k}\int h_{l}(x_{l})p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(x_{k},x_{-k})d\nu_{-k}(x_{-k})
for
k\in[m]
. Such an inverse
\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}
exists by Lemma 3.2 in
[
21
]
; see also more details in the proof in Appendix
F
.

Theorem 4.6
(CLT for Schrödinger coupling).

If the cost function
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
and
g\in L^{\infty}(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j})
such that
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(g)>0
, then we have that as
N\to\infty
,

\sqrt{N}\left(\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon}(g)-{\pi}^{*}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)\overset{w}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(g)),

where

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(g)=(m-1)^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\text{Var}(\bar{\phi}^{(j)}(X_{1}^{(j)})),

with

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}^{(j^{*})}(x)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{K}(X^{(j)}_{\beta},\beta=1,\dots,m-1,j\in[m]\backslash\{j^{*}\};x,X^{(j^{*})}_{2},\dots,X^{(j^{*})}_{m-1})\right],

for
m
independent sequences
X_{1}^{(j)},\dots,X_{m-1}^{(j)}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\nu_{j}
,
j\in[m]
of independent random variables.

Remark 4.7
(Non-degeneracy of the limiting variance).

The asymptotic variance
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(g)=0

if and only if

\mathbb{E}_{(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})\sim\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}}[g(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})|X_{j}]=\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}(g)\quad\text{for all}\quad j\in[m].

The verification details can be found in Appendix
F
.

The proof of Theorem
4.6
is quite involved and consists of seven steps we sketch below. To circumvent the missing regularity, we begin by linearizing the empirical EMOT system, relying on the concentration estimates established in Appendix
C
. We then introduce several auxiliary operators and derive precise operator-norm bounds to control the resulting approximation errors. This analysis ultimately reveals that the objective can be reduces to a multi-sample
V
-statistic up to asymptotically negligible terms. The asymptotic variance
\bar{\sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(g)
in Theorem
4.6
depends on linear (a.k.a. Hoeffding) projection of the kernel function
\mathcal{K}
in (
19
). The first term of
\mathcal{K}
quantifies the symmetrized fluctuation of the empirical Schrödinger potentials and the second term captures the marginal fluctuation of the centered empirical sum of empirical distributions, both weighted by the Gibbs kernel in the dual geometry (
5
). The proof details are given in the Appendix
F
.

We remark that the type of weak limit
\sqrt{N}\left(\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon}-{\pi}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\right)
was firstly proposed with a modified empirical estimator for Schrödinger’s lazy gas experiment and also conjectured to be valid in EOT setting (i.e.,
m=2
) in
[
53
]
. In
[
47
]
, the conjectured was proven in the entropic OT setting with smooth cost function and
[
48
]
provided corresponding results for nonsmooth case. Recently,
[
46
]
obtained this pointwise weak limit in the sparse regularized OT problem. Moreover, using quite a different approach,
[
44
]
investigated the weak limit by establishing Hadamard differentiability. Additionally,
[
45
]
developed a unified framework for deriving limit distributions of empirical proxies of various regularized OT distances, semiparametric efficiency of the plug-in empirical estimator and bootstrap consistency.
For comparison, the two-marginal CLT for Schrödinger coupling established in
[
48
]
, under bounded cost function assumption, relies on the explicit two-marginal resolvent representation
of the linearized Schrödinger system. After normalizing the second potential, the linearized operator acts on

L^{2}(P)\times L^{2}_{0}(Q)
and takes the form

\bm{\Gamma}=\begin{pmatrix}I&\mathcal{A}_{Q}\\
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{P}&I\end{pmatrix},\qquad\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{P}h:=\mathcal{A}_{P}h-\int\mathcal{A}_{P}h\,dQ.

Its inverse admits the representation

\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}(I-\mathcal{A}_{Q}\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{P})^{-1}&-(I-\mathcal{A}_{Q}\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{P})^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{Q}\\[6.0pt]
-(I-\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{P}\mathcal{A}_{Q})^{-1}\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{P}&(I-\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{P}\mathcal{A}_{Q})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

The corresponding empirical representation permits approximation
of the inverse by truncated auxiliary Neumann expansions, whose
alternating operator products admit explicit representations as an infinite-order
V
-statistic.

This construction does not extend directly to the general
multimarginal system: operator products involve multiple coordinate
paths, and each empirical pair kernel depends on the remaining
empirical marginals. An analogous auxiliary expansion with the
required approximation and stochastic error bounds would therefore
require additional analysis. We instead take a different route and compare the empirical and
population linearization operators in the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology.
The assumption
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
provides the uniform regularity
bounds needed for the estimates.

In the multimarginal setting,
[
44
]
established the Hadamard differentiability. The only work addressing the pointwise weak limit, apart from ours is
[
50
]
, where they worked under the unbalanced multimarginal optimal transport setting but the exact limit remained unclear there. Our result provides a precise characterization of the pointwise weak limit in the multimarginal setting, thereby extending the results in the two marginal case
[
47
,
53
]
. For more comprehensive review on recent progress in the distributional limit of OT related quantities, see
[
36
]
.

We also would like to mention the following key lemma, which is of independent interest, in the proof of Theorem
4.6
. To the best of our knowledge, Lemma
4.8
provides the first convergence result for the optimal dual potentials in the EMOT problem under
nonsmooth cost function
in the sense of
\mathcal{C}^{2}
.

Lemma 4.8
(Convergence rate for optimal dual potentials of EMOT).

For the given cost function
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{X})
with
\mathcal{X}
compact support,

\displaystyle\|[\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d\log N}{N}}\right).

The proof is deferred to Appendix
D
.
We shall emphasize that most of the existing estimates for empirical Schrödinger potentials
in smooth function norms, either in the EOT or EMOT setting, relies heavily on higher-order smoothness assumptions on the cost function than
\mathcal{C}^{2}
, to mitigate the curse-of-dimensionality. Under the smooth cost function assumption
c\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, in the two marginal EOT problem,
[
37
]
showed that for
s\geq d/2+1
,

\displaystyle\|[\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{s}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(N^{-1/2}\right).

Suppose
c\in\mathcal{C}^{s+p}
with
p>d/2
,
[
21
]
established that for EMOT potential,

\displaystyle\|[\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{s}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(N^{-1/2}\right).

Our Lemma
4.8
indicates that the dimension-independent regularity assumption

c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
suffices for a multimarginal estimate in the

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
norm, with rate

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{d\log N/N})
.

Another line of work established convergence in
L^{2}
norms for normalized two-marginal potentials.
For fixed
\varepsilon>0
,

[
79
]

established an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1/2})
rate under bounded costs assumption,
with constants depending exponentially on
1/\varepsilon
.
Refining this approach,
[
85
]
obtained
the same rate in
N
,
with polynomial dependence on
1/\varepsilon
governed by
intrinsic dimension, under bounded uniformly Lipschitz costs
and additional manifold and density assumptions.

4.3
Bootstrap inference

Despite we have obtained the limiting distribution for EMOT related objects in Theorem
4.5
and Theorem
4.6
, one limitation is that the desired weak limit is non-pivotal, in the sense that it depends on the unknown population distribution
\nu_{j}
,
j\in[m]
in a highly nonlinear manner. In this section, we consider the classical empirical bootstrap procedure described below, serving as a remedy for this obstacle.

For
j\in[m]
, given data
X_{1}^{(j)},\dots,X_{N}^{(j)}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\nu_{j}
, let
X_{1}^{(j),B},\dots,X_{N}^{(j),B}
be an independent sample from
\hat{\nu}_{j}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{k}^{(j)}}
and set
\hat{\nu}^{B}_{j}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{k}^{(j),B}}
as the bootstrap empirical distribution. We denote
\hat{\pi}^{B}_{\varepsilon}
as the Schrödinger coupling computed from the empirical bootstrap distributions
\hat{\nu}^{B}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{B}_{m}
. Let
\mathbb{P}^{B}
denote the conditional probability given the data. The following two theorems establish the bootstrap validity for cost functional and Schrödinger coupling under the Kolmogorov metric.

Theorem 4.9
(Bootstrap validity for cost functional).

Suppose the cost function
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2k}
with
k\geq d/2+1
and
\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}>0
. Then we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{P}^{B}\left(\sqrt{N}\left(S_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\nu}^{B}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{B}_{m})-S_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m})\right)\leq t\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{N}\left(S_{\varepsilon}(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m})-S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})\right)\leq t\right)\Big|\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}0.

Theorem 4.10
(Bootstrap validity for Schrödinger coupling).

Suppose the cost function
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2k}
for
k\geq\frac{d}{2}+1
. If
\bar{\sigma}^{2}_{\varepsilon}(g)>0
for
g\in\mathcal{C}^{k}
, then we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}^{B}\left(\sqrt{N}\left(\hat{\pi}^{B}_{\varepsilon}(g)-\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)\leq t\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{N}\left(\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon}(g)-\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon}(g)\right)\leq t\right)\right|\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}0.

Note that in Theorems
4.9
and
4.10
, we require a slightly stronger smoothness condition on cost function than the weak limit results in Section
4.2
. This is partially due to the steps of the Hadamard differentiability and functional delta method are intensely used in the proof. A quick review of them along with the proof of the theorems above can be found in Appendix
K
. See
[
89
,
88
,
80
,
44
,
45
]
for more detailed introduction and applications. We shall leave the bootstrap consistency under
\mathcal{C}^{2}
as an open problem.
We provide numerical verification for bootstrap consistency in Appendix
L
.

5
Multimarginal Schrödinger barycenter

In this section, we demonstrate how results derived in Section
4
provide statistical guarantees for the barycenter inference. The Wasserstein barycenter, introduced by
[
2
]
, has been widely considered as a natural model under the optimal transport metric for averaging measure-valued data. Given
m
probability measures
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
with support
\mathcal{X}_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
j\in[m]
with finite second moments and a barycentric coordinate vector
\alpha:=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m})
such that
\alpha_{j}\geq 0
and
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{m}=1
, the Wasserstein barycenter
\bar{\nu}
is defined as a solution of minimizing the weighted variance functional

\inf_{\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}W_{2}^{2}(\nu_{i},\nu),

(20)

where
W_{2}(\mu,\nu)
denotes the 2-Wasserstein distance between
\mu
and
\nu
.

For (discrete) probability measures with finite support of size
N
(e.g., empirical measures on
N
-point clouds), their Wasserstein barycenter can be computed in poly
(m,N)
-time when the underlying domain dimension
d
is fixed
[
6
]
, while computing (or even constant-accuracy approximating) the Wasserstein barycenter is NP-hard for general dimension
[
7
]
. On the other hand, quantitative statistical guarantees for estimating Wasserstein barycenters when we only have access to
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
via their empirical analogs as random point clouds are far from being well-understood with a few exceptions by making strong curvature assumptions
[
3
,
49
]
.

It is known that the MOT and Wasserstein barycenter problems are closely connected. Specifically,
[
2
]
shows that the barycenter
\overline{\nu}
can be recovered from the MOT solution
\pi_{0}^{*}
of (
1
) via

\bar{\nu}:=\bar{\nu}_{0}={(T_{\alpha})}_{\sharp}\pi_{0}^{*},

for
c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=c_{\alpha}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}):=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}|\alpha_{i}x_{i}-\alpha_{j}x_{j}|^{2}
, where
T_{\alpha}(\bm{x})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}x_{j}
for
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
. Here, the pushforward measure
T_{\sharp}\mu
of a source probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
for a measurable map
T:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, is defined by
(T_{\sharp}\mu)(B)=\mu(T^{-1}(B))
for measurable subset
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
.

Given the fundamental limitation of computing, estimating and performing inference on the exact barycenter solution in (
20
), we propose a new notion of entropic barycenter for averaging probability measures based on the EMOT formulation (
2
). Our primary goal is to study its statistical properties, including the sample complexity for estimating several key quantities (cost functional, coupling, barycenter) based on point clouds data sampled from the marginal distributions, as well as weak limits of these quantities of interest.

Below, we introduce the
multimarginal Schrödinger barycenter
(MSB) based on the EMOT formulation, which admits fast linear time complexity off-the-shelf algorithms to compute
[
19
,
66
]
. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 5.1
.

Given a collection of probability measures
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
, the
multimarginal Schrödinger barycenter
(MSB)
\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
is defined as

\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}={(T_{\alpha})}_{\sharp}\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*},

(21)

where
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}
is the unique minimizer of the EMOT problem

S_{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})}\Big\{\int_{\mathcal{X}}c_{\alpha}d\pi+\varepsilon\ \text{KL}(\pi||\nu_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\nu_{m})\Big\},

(22)

with the cost function
c_{\alpha}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}|\alpha_{i}x_{i}-\alpha_{j}x_{j}|^{2}
.

Compared to existing regularized barycenters, MSB strikes a careful balance offering enough regularization for statistical and computational gains without significantly distorting the original barycenter. One could examine this by checking the different notions of regularized barycenters of
m
Dirac masses. This advantage of manipulating regularization on multimarginal formulation over pairwise regularization has also been empirically observed in a different problem
[
51
]
. The following basic properties of MSB could be easily derived and their proofs are deferred to Appendix
B
and Appendix
I
.

Proposition 5.2
(Approximation).

Any cluster point of
(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is a Wasserstein barycenter
\bar{\nu}
. If further
\bar{\nu}
is unique, then we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}W_{2}(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\bar{\nu})=0.

Proposition 5.3
(Lipschitz continuity).

Let
\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
) be the MSB for
\bm{\nu}:=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})\in\Pi_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X}_{j})
(resp.
\bm{\tilde{\nu}}:=(\tilde{\nu}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\nu}_{m})\in\Pi_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X}_{j})
). We have

W_{1}(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon})\leq\sqrt{m}W_{2}(\bm{\nu},\bm{\tilde{\nu}})+CW_{2}(\bm{\nu},\bm{\tilde{\nu}})^{1/2},

where
W_{2}(\bm{\nu},\bm{\tilde{\nu}}):=(\sum_{j=1}^{m}W_{2}(\nu_{j},\tilde{\nu}_{j})^{2})^{1/2}
and
C>0
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon
, and the second moment of
\nu_{j}
and
\tilde{\nu}_{j}
for
j\in[m]
.

5.1
Statistical guarantees

Given the samples
\{X^{(j)}_{i}\}_{j\in[m],i\in[N]}
from the standard sampling model, the empirical MSB
\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon}
is defined as
\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon}=({T_{\alpha}})_{\sharp}\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon}
where
\hat{\pi}^{N}_{\varepsilon}
is the unique minimizer of the following problem

S_{\alpha,\varepsilon}(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m}):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m})}\int_{\mathcal{X}}c_{\alpha}d\pi+\varepsilon\text{KL}(\pi||\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m}).

(23)

Corollary 5.4
(Concentration for empirical MSB).

For
\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon}
) the (resp. empirical) multimarginal Schrödinger barycenter, there exists a constant
C:=C(m,\varepsilon)>0
such that for any
h\in L^{\infty}(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon})
, and for all
t>0
, we have with probability at least
1-(2m^{2}-2m+2)e^{-t}
,

|(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}-\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon})(h)|\leq C\|h\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}.

(24)

It comes obvious by setting
g=h\circ T_{\alpha}
in Theorem
4.3
.
∎

Furthermore, we quantify the convergence rate of MSB over the
\beta
-smooth Hölder function class
\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}([-1,1]^{d};\beta,L)
as considered in Theorem
4.4
.

Theorem 5.5
(Barycenter convergence rate on Hölder class).

There exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
m,\varepsilon,\beta,\|c\|_{\infty}
and
d
such that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}|(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}-\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon})(h)|\leq\begin{cases}CLN^{-1/2}&\text{if}\quad d<2\beta,\\
CLN^{-1/2}\log{N}&\text{if}\quad d=2\beta,\\
CLN^{-\beta/d}&\text{if}\quad d>2\beta.\end{cases}

(25)

In particular, we have the following expectation bound on the
p
-Wasserstein distances for
p=1,2
:

\mathbb{E}[W_{p}^{p}(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon})]\leq\begin{cases}CN^{-1/2}&\text{if}\quad d<2p,\\
CN^{-1/2}\log{N}&\text{if}\quad d=2p,\\
CN^{-p/d}&\text{if}\quad d>2p,\end{cases}

(26)

for some constant
C>0
depending only on
m,\varepsilon,d,\|c\|_{\infty}
.

The proof of Theorem
5.5
is relegated to Appendix
D
.

Remark 5.6
.

We highlight that our convergence rate (
26
) is sharp up to poly-log terms and in general cannot be improved without extra structural assumptions on
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}
. To see this, consider the case
m=1
, or equivalently
m=2
and
\alpha=(1,0)
. Then the MSB degenerates to the empirical measure on the point cloud
X^{(1)}_{1},\dots,X^{(1)}_{N}
, which is known to have the optimal convergence rate under the Wasserstein distance
W_{p}
for all
p\geq 1

[
93
,
41
]
.

In addition, we make comparisons with existing literature. Using the concept of shadow, Theorem 3.3 in
[
9
]
implies that
W_{1}(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon})\leq m^{1/2}\Delta_{N}+C\Delta_{N}^{1/2}
, where
\Delta_{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}W_{1}(\nu_{j},\hat{\nu}^{N}_{j})
. Substituting the (optimal)
W_{1}
rate of convergence of the empirical measure (e.g., Theorem 1 in
[
41
]
) into the last inequality, one gets

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}W_{1}(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon})\leq\begin{cases}CN^{-1/4},&\quad\text{if}\quad d=1,\\
CN^{-1/4}(\log{N})^{1/2},&\quad\text{if}\quad d=2,\\
CN^{-1/2d},&\quad\text{if}\quad d>3,\\
\end{cases}

(27)

for some constant
C>0
depending only on
m,\varepsilon,d
.
In comparison, our rate (
26
) specialized to
p=1
substantially improves (
27
) for every dimension
d\geq 1
.

Next, thanks to Theorem
4.6
, we are ready for the weak limit of the expectation of a bounded test function with respect to MSB, further indicating the advantage of our proposed notion of barycenter.

Corollary 5.7
(CLT for MSB).

For
h\in L^{\infty}(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon})
with
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}(h)>0
, it holds true that

\sqrt{N}\left(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon}(h)-\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(h)\right)\overset{w}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}(h)),

(28)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}(h)=(m-1)^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\text{Var}(\phi^{(j)}(X_{1}^{(j)})),

with

\displaystyle\phi^{(j^{*})}(x)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{K}_{0}(X^{(j)}_{\beta},\beta=1,\dots,m-1,j\in[m]\backslash\{j^{*}\};x,X^{(j^{*})}_{2},\dots,X^{(j^{*})}_{m-1})\right],

for
m
independent sequences
X_{1}^{(j)},\dots,X_{m-1}^{(j)}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\nu_{j}
,
j\in[m]
of independent random variables. Here,

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{K}_{0}(y^{(j)}_{\beta},\beta=i_{j,1},\dots,i_{j,m-1};j=1,\dots,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left((m-1)!\right)^{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi_{j}\in S_{m-1}\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\Psi_{0}(y^{(j)}_{\beta},\beta=i_{j,\pi_{j}(1)},\dots,i_{j,\pi_{j}(m-1)};j=1,\dots,m)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{m-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{t=1}^{m-1}\left[(\nu_{-j})(\tilde{h}_{\alpha}\,p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(y_{i_{j,t}}^{(j)})-(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})(\tilde{h}_{\alpha}\,p^{*}_{\varepsilon})\right],

where
S_{m-1}
denotes all the permutation on
\{1,\dots,m-1\}
,
\tilde{h}_{\alpha}=h\circ T_{\alpha}
, and

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}(y^{(j)}_{\alpha},\alpha=i_{j,1},\dots,i_{j,m-1};j=1,\dots,m)

\displaystyle:=-\mathop{\text{$\int$}}\nolimits\bigoplus\left(\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1},y^{(2)}_{i_{2,1}},\dots,y^{(m)}_{i_{m,1}})-1\\
p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(y^{(1)}_{i_{1,1}},x_{2},\dots,y^{(m)}_{i_{m,2}})-1\\
\vdots\\
p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(y^{(1)}_{i_{1,m-2}},y^{(2)}_{i_{2,m-2}},\dots,x_{m-1},y^{(m)}_{i_{m,m-1}})-1\\
p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(y^{(1)}_{i_{1,m-1}},y^{(2)}_{i_{2,m-1}},\dots,y^{(m-1)}_{i_{m-1,m-1}},x_{m})-1\end{pmatrix}\right)\tilde{h}_{\alpha}(\bm{x})p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x})d(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})(\bm{x}).

It comes obvious by setting
g=h\circ T_{\alpha}
in Theorem
4.6
.
∎

Finally, we provide the bootstrap consistency for MSB. Suppose
\hat{\nu}^{B}_{\varepsilon}
is the MSB computed from the empirical bootstrap distributions
\hat{\nu}^{B}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{B}_{m}
.

Corollary 5.8
(Bootstrap validity for MSB).

Provided that
\sigma^{2}_{\varepsilon}(h)>0
for
h\in\mathcal{C}^{s}([-1,1]^{d})
with
s\geq 1+d/2
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}^{B}\left(\sqrt{N}\left(\hat{\nu}^{B}_{\varepsilon}(h)-\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon}(h)\right)\leq t\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{N}\left(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{\varepsilon}(h)-\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(h)\right)\leq t\right)\right|\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\longrightarrow}0.

It comes obvious by setting
g=h\circ T_{\alpha}
in Theorem
4.10
.
∎

Appendix

The appendix is organized as follows.

•

Appendix
A
introduces additional notations used throughout the appendix section.

•

Appendix
B
presents proof of approximation error of EMOT as the regularization
\varepsilon\to 0
.

•

Appendix
C
focuses on the high probability bound on the empirical gradient norm
\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}^{*})\|_{\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{m}}
, one of the central quantity for the analysis.

•

Appendix
D
develops detailed properties about population and empirical optimal Schrödinger potentials
f^{*}_{j},\hat{f}^{*}_{j},\,j\in[m]
along with proof of Theorem
4.3
, Lemma
4.8
and Theorem
5.5
.

•

Appendix
E
contains proofs of results related to the cost functional, namely proof of Theorem
4.1
(sample complexity) and proof of Theorem
4.5
(weak limit).

•

Appendix
F
outlines the proof structure of Theorem
4.6
, giving weak limit of expectation under entropic optimal transport coupling.

•

Appendices
G
and
H

complete the detailed technical steps deferred from Appendix
F
.

•

Appendix
I
collects the remaining proofs of the main theorems.

•

Appendix
J
gathers auxiliary technical lemmas.

•

Appendix
K
concerns the Hadamard differentiability used for establishing bootstrap consistency.

•

Appendix
L
reports the numerical verification of bootstrap consistency.

A
List of notations

For any parameter
\varepsilon
, we use
x\lesssim_{\varepsilon}y
(resp.
x\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}y
) to denote
x\leq C_{\varepsilon}y
(resp.
x\geq C_{\varepsilon}y
) for some constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
depending on
\varepsilon
. If
x\lesssim_{\varepsilon}y
and
x\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}y
, we write
x\asymp_{\varepsilon}y
. For
f_{j}:\cal X\to\mathbb{R}
, we shall denote a vector-valued function using the boldface
\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})
. We shall also denote a vector
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\|\bm{x}\|_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}^{2}}
,
\|\bm{x}\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|x_{j}|
,
and
\bm{x}^{T}
denotes the transpose of a vector. For a vector space
V
over some field
\mathbb{F}
, the dual space of
V
, denoted by
V^{\prime}
, is the set of all linear functionals from
V
to
\mathbb{F}
.

Given a set
\mathcal{D}
,
|\mathcal{D}|
denotes the cardinality of
\mathcal{D}
. We define the diameter of a set
S
in a metric space with distance
d
as
\text{diam}(S)=\sup_{x,y\in S}d(x,y)
.
a\vee b:=\max\{a,b\}
. For
f:\mathcal{X}_{2}\to\mathcal{X}_{3}
and
g:\mathcal{X}_{1}\to\mathcal{X}_{2}
, the compound function
f\circ g:\mathcal{X}_{1}\to\mathcal{X}_{3}
is defined as
f\circ g(x):=f(g(x))
. For a function class
\mathcal{F}
,
N(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon,\|\cdot\|)
denotes the
\varepsilon
-covering number of class
\mathcal{F}
under the metric induced by
\|\cdot\|
. Given
\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X}_{j})
,
\bigoplus(f_{1},\dots,f_{m}):=\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}
. Often
\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(x_{i})
is written as
f_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus f_{m}
. For
f_{j}:\mathcal{X}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\nabla f_{j}:\mathcal{X}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes its gradient and
\nabla\mathbf{f}=(\nabla f_{1},\dots,\nabla f_{m})
.

We sometimes consider the
\beta
-smooth Hölder function class
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{X};\beta,L)
containing the set of functions
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\|f\|_{\beta}:=\max_{|k|\leq\lfloor\beta\rfloor}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|D^{k}f(x)|+\max_{|k|=\lfloor\beta\rfloor}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\neq y\\
x,y\in\mathcal{X}\end{subarray}}\frac{|D^{k}f(x)-D^{k}f(y)|}{\|x-y\|^{\beta-\lfloor\beta\rfloor}}\leq L

for some parameter
L>0
, where
\lfloor\beta\rfloor
is the largest integer that is strictly smaller than
\beta
. Here, for a multi-index
k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{m})
of
m
integers,
|k|=\sum_{j=1}^{m}k_{j}
and the differential operator

D^{k}:=\frac{\partial^{|k|}}{\partial x_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{m}^{k_{m}}}.
Note that
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{X};1,L)
is the class of Lipschitz continuous functions on
\mathcal{X}
with constant
L
.

We say
a_{n}=\mathcal{O}(b_{n})
if
\limsup_{n\to\infty}|\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}|<\infty
.
For a sequence of random variables
(\xi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and a sequence of scalars
a_{n}>0
, we write
\xi_{n}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(a_{n})

if for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{|\xi_{n}|}{a_{n}}>\varepsilon\right)=0.
We write
\xi_{n}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(a_{n})

if for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
M>0
such that
\sup_{n}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{|\xi_{n}|}{a_{n}}>M\right)<\varepsilon.

We use
\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\Omega)
) to denote all the probability measures on
\Omega
(resp. all the probability measures on
\Omega
with finite second moment). For
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a positive definite matrix
\Sigma
,
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
represents the Gaussian distribution with mean vector
\mu
and covariance matrix
\Sigma
. Let
(\xi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of random variables taking values in a Polish space
\Xi
, and let
\xi
be another random variable on
\Xi
. We say that
\xi_{n}
converges weakly to
\xi
, and write
\xi_{n}\overset{w}{\longrightarrow}\xi

if the sequence of laws
(\mathcal{L}(\xi_{n}))_{n\geq 1}
converges weakly to the law
\mathcal{L}(\xi)
. Equivalently, for every bounded continuous function
f:\Xi\to\mathbb{R}
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[f(\xi_{n})]\;=\;\mathbb{E}[f(\xi)].

B
Approximation error

Recall that we suppose that
\nu_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is compactly supported on
\mathcal{X}_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
j\in[m]
. Let
d_{0}
be the Euclidean distance in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We endow
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{X}_{j})
with the topology induced by the Wasserstein metric
W_{2}
. It is known (see
[
91
]
) that in this compact support setting
W_{2}
metrizes the weak topology (namely,
Q_{n}\to Q
if and only if
\int fdQ_{n}\to\int fdQ
for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{b}(\mathcal{X}_{j})
).
Assume that
c
is continuous.
Define the functionals

C_{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{P}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j}\right)\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,

C_{\varepsilon}(\pi)=\begin{cases}\int c\,d\pi+\varepsilon\text{KL}(\pi||\nu_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\nu_{m})&\text{if }\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}),\\
\infty&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

and functional
C_{0}:\mathcal{P}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j}\right)\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,

C_{0}(\pi)=\begin{cases}\int c\,d\pi&\text{if }\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}),\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Obviously,
S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})}C_{\varepsilon}(\pi)
and
S_{0}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})}C_{0}(\pi)
.

Definition B.1
(
\Gamma-
convergence).

A sequence of functional
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
is said to
\Gamma
-converge
to
\mathcal{F}
if the following two conditions hold for every
x
,

1.

for any sequence
x_{\varepsilon}
converging to
x
,
\mathcal{F}(x)\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})
,

2.

there exists a sequence
x_{\varepsilon}
converging to
x
,
\mathcal{F}(x)\geq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})
.

For equivalent definitions and other more background information on
\Gamma
-convergence, we refer the reader to
[
12
]
.

Proposition B.2
.

The sequence
(C_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}

\Gamma
-converges to
C_{0}
w.r.t. the weak topology.

The proof follows from Lemma
B.3
and Lemma
B.4
.
∎

Lemma B.3
(liminf inequality).

Let
\pi_{\varepsilon}
be an arbitrary sequence in
\mathcal{P}(\Pi_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j})
that converges to
\pi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Pi_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j})
. Then

C_{0}(\pi_{0})\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}C_{\varepsilon}(\pi_{\varepsilon}).

Lemma B.4
(limsup inequality).

For every
\pi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Pi_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j})
,
there exists a sequence
\pi_{\varepsilon}\in

\mathcal{P}(\Pi_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j})
converging to
\pi_{0}
, such that

C_{0}(\pi_{0})\geq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}C_{\varepsilon}(\pi_{\varepsilon}).

Since
\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
is tight via Lemma
J.1
and weakly closed, we have the compactness via Prohorov’s theorem. Hence,
C_{\varepsilon}:\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})\to\mathbb{R}
is equi-coercive: for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the set
\{\pi\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}),C_{\varepsilon}(\pi)\leq t\}
is included in some compact set
K_{t}
. Indeed, the set above is closed in the topology induced by the Wasserstein metric.

Combining with the
\Gamma-
convergence in Proposition
B.2
, based on Theorem 2.10 in
[
12
]
, we have that any cluster point of
\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon}
, the optimizer of
C_{\varepsilon}
, is a minimizer of
C_{0}
. All the results follow.
∎

Since
\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
is weakly closed, if
\pi_{0}\notin\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
, we will eventually have
\pi_{\varepsilon}\notin\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Thus
C_{\varepsilon}(\pi_{\varepsilon})=C_{0}(\pi_{0})=\infty
. So we assume
\pi_{0}\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
from now on.
By the definition of the topology induced by the Wasserstein metric here and the non-negativity of the entropy term, we obtain that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}C_{\varepsilon}(\pi_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int cd\pi_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\text{KL}(\pi_{\varepsilon}||\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j})\geq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int cd\pi_{\varepsilon}=C_{0}(\pi_{0}).

∎

To prove Lemma
B.4
, we essentially follow the block approximation technique that was used to show the convergence rate of two marginal entropic optimal transport costs
[
26
]
. Recently,
[
70
]
adapted this method to multimarginal problems and gained the convergence rate of the corresponding entropic optimal transport costs. As mentioned in
[
70
]
, the
\Gamma
-convergence of multimarginal entropic optimal transport costs is clear by the block approximation method. Here for completeness, we write down our proof details of Lemma
B.4
.

Take
\pi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j})
. If
\pi_{0}\notin\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
, take
\pi_{\varepsilon}=\pi_{0}
and the conclusion follows. Henceforth, assume
\pi_{0}\in\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
.
For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
i\in[m]
, consider a partition

\mathcal{X}_{i}=\bigsqcup_{1\leq n\leq L_{i}}A_{i}^{n}

of Borel sets such that
\operatorname{diam}(A_{i}^{n})\leq\varepsilon
for every
1\leq n\leq L_{i}
, with
L_{i}=L_{i}(\varepsilon)\lesssim(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{d}
due to the compactness of
\mathcal{X}_{i}
. Also, set

\nu_{i}^{n}:=\begin{cases}\frac{\nu_{i}\lfloor_{A_{i}^{n}}}{\nu_{i}(A_{i}^{n})},&\text{if }\nu_{i}(A_{i}^{n})>0,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\mu\lfloor A
means the restriction of the Borel measure
\mu
to the Borel set
A
defined by
\mu\lfloor A(E):=\mu(A\cap E)
for every
E
. Then for every
m
-tuple
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{m})\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}[L_{j}]
, define

(\pi_{0})^{n}:=\pi_{0}(A_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\dots\times A_{m}^{n_{m}})\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu^{n_{k}}_{k}.

Finally define

\pi_{\varepsilon}:=\sum_{n\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}[L_{j}]}(\pi_{0})^{n}.

By definition,
\pi_{\varepsilon}\ll\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\nu_{i}
and it is easily checked that
{e_{i}}_{\sharp}\pi_{\varepsilon}=\nu_{i}
, for
i\in[m]
. Based on the construction above, we also know that

W_{2}(\pi_{0},\pi_{\varepsilon})\leq m\varepsilon,

equipping the
\Pi_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j}
with the metric
D((z_{1},\dots,z_{m}),(z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,z^{\prime}_{m})):=\sum_{j=1}d_{0}(z_{j},z^{\prime}_{j})
. As a consequence,
\pi_{\varepsilon}
converges to
\pi_{0}
in the weak topology as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Besides,
\pi_{\varepsilon}(A)=\pi_{0}(A)
for every
A=\Pi_{i=1}^{m}A_{i}^{n_{i}}
where
n\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
, and for
\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\nu_{i}
-almost every
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in\otimes_{i=1}^{m}A_{i}^{n_{i}}
,

\frac{d\pi_{\varepsilon}}{d\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\nu_{i}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}):=\begin{cases}\frac{\pi_{0}(A_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\dots\times A_{m}^{n_{m}})}{\nu_{1}(A_{1}^{n_{1}})\cdots\nu_{m}(A_{m}^{n_{m}})},&\text{if }\nu_{1}(A_{1}^{n_{1}})\cdots\nu_{m}(A_{m}^{n_{m}})>0,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For the entropy term, we have

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\pi_{\varepsilon}|\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\nu_{i})=\sum_{n\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}[L_{j}]}\int_{\prod_{i=1}^{m}A^{n_{i}}_{i}}\log\left(\frac{\pi_{0}(A^{n_{1}}_{1}\times\dots\times A^{n_{m}}_{m})}{\nu_{1}(A^{n_{1}}_{1})\cdots\nu_{m}(A^{n_{m}}_{m})}\right)d\pi_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}[L_{j}]}\pi_{0}(A^{n_{1}}_{1}\times\dots\times A^{n_{m}}_{m})\log\left(\frac{\pi_{0}(A^{n_{1}}_{1}\times\dots\times A^{n_{m}}_{m})}{\nu_{1}(A^{n_{1}}_{1})\cdots\nu_{m}(A^{n_{m}}_{m})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}[L_{j}]}\pi_{0}(A^{n_{1}}_{1}\times\dots\times A^{n_{m}}_{m})\log\left(\frac{\pi_{0}(A^{n_{1}}_{1}\times\dots\times A^{n_{m}}_{m})}{\nu_{m}(A^{n_{m}}_{m})}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\sum_{n\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}[L_{j}]}\pi_{0}(A^{n_{1}}_{1}\times\dots\times A^{n_{m}}_{m})\log(1/\nu_{j}(A^{n_{j}}_{j}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}[L_{j}]}\pi_{0}(A_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\dots\times A_{m}^{n_{m}})\log\left(\frac{\pi_{0}(A_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\dots\times A_{m}^{n_{m}})}{\nu_{m}(A_{m}^{n_{m}})}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\sum_{n_{j}\in[L_{j}]}\pi_{0}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j-1}\mathcal{X}_{i}\times A_{j}^{n_{j}}\times\prod_{i=j+1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i}\right)\log(1/\nu_{j}(A_{j}^{n_{j}}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\sum_{n_{j}\in[L_{j}]}\nu_{j}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})\log(1/\nu_{j}(A_{j}^{n_{j}})).

where the last inequality comes from the inequality

\pi_{0}(A_{1}^{n_{1}}\times\dots\times A_{m}^{n_{m}})\leq\nu_{m}(A_{m}^{n_{m}})
.
By the concavity of
t\mapsto t\log(1/t)
, Jensen’s inequality gives

\sum_{1\leq n_{j}\leq L_{j}}\nu_{j}(A^{n_{j}}_{j})\log(1/\nu_{j}(A^{n_{j}}_{j}))\leq\log(L_{j}).

Thus, we get

\text{KL}(\pi_{\varepsilon}|\otimes_{i=1}^{m}\nu_{i})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\log L_{j}.

All the analysis above yields that

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}(\pi_{\varepsilon})\leq\int cd\pi_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\log L_{j}.

By the compactness of
\mathcal{X}_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we know that
\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\log L_{j}\lesssim md\varepsilon\log(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. As a consequence, we obtain that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}C_{\varepsilon}(\pi_{\varepsilon})\leq C_{0}(\pi_{0}).

∎

C
Concentration bound on the empirical gradient norm

This section is devoted to a high probability bound on the empirical gradient norm
\|\nabla\widehat{\Phi}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}^{*})\|_{\widehat{\mathscr{L}}_{m}}
, which is fundamental to our main results. Specifically, our goal is to prove the following Lemma
C.1
. We present our results in a more general setting as in Lemma
C.1
under the following assumptions, which might be of broader usage for multimarginal system analysis. Lemma
C.1
offers new technical insights and improvements on the existing literature concerning the multimarginal regularized optimal transport problem.

Assumption 1
.

We have access to
m
independent sequence
X^{(j)}_{1},\dots,X^{(j)}_{N}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\nu_{j}
for
\nu_{j}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,j\in[m]
of independent random variables.

Assumption 2
.

Suppose
c:\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
is bounded.

Assumption 3
.

Suppose
p_{\varepsilon}^{*}=\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}^{*}-c}{\varepsilon}\right):\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}\,
for
\,f^{*}_{j}:\mathcal{X}_{j}\to\mathbb{R},j\in[m]

satisfies for
j\in[m]
,

\displaystyle\int p_{\varepsilon}^{*}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})d\nu_{-j}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=1.

(29)

Lemma C.1
(Concentration of empirical gradient norm).

Under Assumption (
1
)-(
3
), for all
t>0
, we have with probability at least
1-2m(m-1)e^{-t}
,
