Since each
\mathcal{X}_{j}
is compact, the derivatives
\partial_{x_{i,\ell}}c
are continuous and bounded:

L_{i}:=\|\nabla_{x_{i}}c\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}|\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}c(\bm{x})|<\infty,\qquad L:=\sum_{1\leq i\leq m}L_{i}<\infty.

We consider the Banach space

\mathcal{C}^{1}:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{j}).

For
\bm{\phi}=(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m})\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
i\in[m]
, define

\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})(x_{i}):=\phi_{i}(x_{i})+\varepsilon\log\!\int\exp\!\Big(\frac{\sum_{j\neq i}\phi_{j}(x_{j})}{\varepsilon}\Big)\,\mathbb{C}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\,d\widehat{\nu}_{-i}^{\,N}(x_{-i}),

(89)

and set
\widehat{T}(\bm{\phi}):=(\widehat{T}_{1}(\bm{\phi}),\dots,\widehat{T}_{m}(\bm{\phi}))
. All the upcoming integrals and differentiations make sense due to the regularity we have.

Proposition G.1
(First and second Fréchet derivative).

(a)

\widehat{T}:\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}
satisfies that

\nabla_{x_{i}}\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})(x_{i})=\nabla_{x_{i}}\phi_{i}(x_{i})+\varepsilon\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}\!\big[\nabla_{x_{i}}\log\mathbb{C}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\big]=\nabla_{x_{i}}\phi_{i}(x_{i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}\!\big[\nabla_{x_{i}}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\big],

where

\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}(dx_{-i}):=\frac{\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j\neq i}\phi_{j}(x_{j})}{\varepsilon}\right)\mathbb{C}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})}{\int\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j\neq i}\phi_{j}(x_{j})}{\varepsilon}\right)\mathbb{C}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\,d\widehat{\nu}_{-i}^{\,N}(x_{-i})}\,d\widehat{\nu}_{-i}^{\,N}(x_{-i}).

Thus,

\|\nabla_{x_{i}}\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})\|_{\infty}\leq\|\nabla_{x_{i}}\phi_{i}\|_{\infty}+L_{i}.

(b)

For
\bm{\phi}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
\bm{h}=(h_{1},\dots,h_{m})\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
,

D\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})[\bm{h}](x_{i})=h_{i}(x_{i})+\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}\!\big[h_{j}(X_{j})\big].

(90)

Hence,

\|D\widehat{T}(\bm{\phi})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max\{m,\,1+2(m-1)L/\varepsilon\}.

(c)

For
(\bm{h}^{(1)},\bm{h}^{(2)})\in\mathcal{C}^{1}\times\mathcal{C}^{1}
,

\displaystyle D^{2}\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})[(\bm{h}^{(1)},\bm{h}^{(2)})](x_{i})=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{k\neq i}\mathrm{Cov}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}\!\Big(h^{(1)}_{j}(X_{j}),h^{(2)}_{k}(X_{k})\Big).

(91)

Consequently,

\|D^{2}\widehat{T}(\bm{\phi})\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1},\,\mathcal{C}^{1})\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max\left\{\frac{2(m-1)^{2}}{\varepsilon},\,\frac{6(m-1)^{2}L}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}.

(a) Direct computation and boundedness of
\nabla_{x_{i}}c
gives the stated result.

(b) Linearization of the log-sum-exp operator (
89
) yields
the first equality, see
[
30
]
for details on log-sum-exp operator. For the gradient, use the
score identity (
93
) with

S^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}:=\nabla_{x_{i}}\log\mathbb{C}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}[\nabla_{x_{i}}\log\mathbb{C}]
:

\nabla_{x_{i}}D\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})[\bm{h}]=\nabla_{x_{i}}h_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}\!\big[h_{j}(X_{j})\,S^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}(X_{-i})\big].

Thus,

\|D\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})[\bm{h}]\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max\Big(m\,\|\bm{h}\|_{\infty},\,\|\nabla h_{i}\|_{\infty}+2(m-1)\frac{L_{i}}{\varepsilon}\,\|\bm{h}\|_{\infty}\Big),

because of
\|S^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2L_{i}/\varepsilon
. Namely,

\|D\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})\|\leq\max\{m,\,1+2(m-1)L/\varepsilon\}.

The claimed bound follows in virtue of Lemma
H.3
.

(c) Linearization of equation (
90
) gives (
91
), see
[
30
]
for properties on log-sum-exp operator. Note that by Lemma
G.3
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{i}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}\!\Big(h^{(1)}_{j}(X_{j}),h^{(2)}_{k}(X_{k})\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}[h^{(1)}_{j}(X_{j})h^{(2)}_{k}(X_{k})S^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}]

\displaystyle-\,\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}[h^{(1)}_{j}(X_{j})]\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}[h^{(2)}_{k}(X_{k})S^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}[h^{(2)}_{k}(X_{k})]\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}}[h^{(1)}_{j}(X_{j})S^{(i,N)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}].

For
(\bm{h}^{(1)},\bm{h}^{(2)})\in\mathcal{C}^{1}\times\mathcal{C}^{1}
, since
\|S^{(i)}_{\bm{\phi},x_{i}}\|_{\infty}\leq 2L_{i}/\varepsilon
,
the following bound is obtained by summing over
j\neq i,k\neq i
.

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x_{i}}D^{2}\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})[(\bm{h}^{(1)},\bm{h}^{(2)})]\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6(m-1)^{2}L_{i}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\,\|\bm{h}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\|\bm{h}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}.

As a result,

\|D^{2}\widehat{T}(\bm{\phi})\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1},\,\mathcal{C}^{1})\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\|D^{2}\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})\|_{(\mathcal{C}^{1},\,\mathcal{C}^{1})\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max\left\{\frac{2(m-1)^{2}}{\varepsilon},\,\frac{6(m-1)^{2}\,L}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}.

∎

Lemma G.2
.

For
\bm{\phi}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
\bm{h}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
, we define
\widehat{R}(\bm{h}):=(\widehat{R}_{1}(\bm{h}),\dots\widehat{R}_{m}(\bm{h}))
, where

\widehat{R}_{i}(\bm{h}):=\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi}+\bm{h})-\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})-D\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi})[\bm{h}]=\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\,D^{2}\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi}+t\bm{h})[\bm{h},\bm{h}]\,dt.

Then we have

\|\widehat{R}(\bm{h})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\|\widehat{R}_{i}(\bm{h})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max\left\{\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{\varepsilon},\,\frac{3(m-1)^{2}L}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\,\|\bm{h}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}^{2}.

(92)

Taylor’s theorem in Banach spaces (see, cf.
[
86
]
) yields the integral remainder formula.
Realizing that

\|D^{2}\widehat{T}_{i}(\bm{\phi}+t\bm{h})[\bm{h},\bm{h}]\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max\{2(m-1)^{2}/\varepsilon,\,6(m-1)^{2}\,L/\varepsilon^{2}\}\|\bm{h}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}^{2}
,
we have

\|\widehat{R}_{i}(\bm{h})\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max\left\{\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{\varepsilon},\,\frac{3(m-1)^{2}L}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\,\|\bm{h}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}^{2}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma G.3
(Differentiation under an exponentially tilted law).

Let
\mu_{j}
be arbitrary probability with compact support
\mathcal{X}_{j}
for
j\in[m]
.
For
k:\mathcal{X}_{i}\times\mathcal{X}_{-i}\to(0,\infty)\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
, each
x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}_{i}
, define

Z(x_{i}):=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{-i}}k(x_{i},x_{-i})\,d\mu_{-i}(x_{-i}).

Define the probability
\pi_{i}(\,\cdot\,|x_{i})
on
\mathcal{X}_{-i}
via

\frac{d\pi_{i}(\,\cdot\,|x_{i})}{d\mu_{-i}}(x_{-i})=\frac{k(x_{i},x_{-i})}{Z(x_{i})}.

For a bounded measurable function
\psi:\mathcal{X}_{-i}\to\mathbb{R}
,
x_{i}\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}(\cdot|x_{i})}[\psi]
is differentiable and

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}(\cdot|x_{i})}[\psi]\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}(\cdot|x_{i})}\big[\psi\,S^{(i)}(x_{i},\cdot)\big],

(93)

with
S^{(i)}(x_{i},x_{-i}):=\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k(x_{i},x_{-i})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}(\cdot|x_{i})}\!\big[\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k(x_{i},\cdot)\big].
Equivalently,

\nabla_{x_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}(\cdot|x_{i})}[\psi]\;=\;\mathrm{Cov}_{\pi_{i}(\cdot|x_{i})}\!\big(\psi,\ \nabla_{x_{i}}\log k(x_{i},\cdot)\big).

We sometimes in the proof write
\pi_{i}(\cdot|x_{i})
as
\pi_{i}
for simplicity. By definition, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}(\cdot|x_{i})}[\psi]=\frac{N(x_{i})}{Z(x_{i})}
, with

N(x_{i}):=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{-i}}\psi(x_{-i})\,k(x_{i},x_{-i})\,d\mu_{-i}(x_{-i}).

Dominated convergence theorem yields

\nabla_{x_{i}}N(x_{i})=\int\psi(x_{-i})\,\nabla_{x_{i}}k(x_{i},x_{-i})\,d\mu_{-i}(x_{-i}),\qquad\nabla_{x_{i}}Z(x_{i})=\int\nabla_{x_{i}}k(x_{i},x_{-i})\,d\mu_{-i}(x_{-i}).

Differentiate the quotient
N(x_{i})/Z(x_{i})
:

\nabla_{x_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\psi]=\frac{\nabla_{x_{i}}N(x_{i})}{Z(x_{i})}-\frac{N(x_{i})}{Z(x_{i})^{2}}\,\nabla_{x_{i}}Z(x_{i})=\frac{1}{Z(x_{i})}\!\int\!\psi\,\nabla_{x_{i}}k\,d\mu_{-i}-\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\psi]\Big)\frac{1}{Z(x_{i})}\!\int\!\nabla_{x_{i}}k\,d\mu_{-i}.

We could rearrange and obtain

\frac{1}{Z(x_{i})}\int\psi\,\nabla_{x_{i}}k\,d\mu_{-i}=\frac{1}{Z(x_{i})}\int\psi\,k\,\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k\,d\mu_{-i}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}\big[\psi\,\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k\big],

and similarly

\frac{1}{Z(x_{i})}\int\nabla_{x_{i}}k\,d\mu_{-i}=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}\big[\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k\big].

Hence

\nabla_{x_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\psi]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}\big[\psi\,\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k\big]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\psi]\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}\big[\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k\big]=\mathrm{Cov}_{\pi_{i}}\!\big(\psi,\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k\big).

Writing
S^{(i)}:=\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k]
gives the stated form

\nabla_{x_{i}}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\psi]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\psi S^{(i)}]
.

∎

Remark G.4
.

If
k(x_{i},x_{-i})=\exp\{s(x_{i},x_{-i})\}
with a differentiable function
s
, then
\nabla_{x_{i}}\log k=\nabla_{x_{i}}s
and the identity becomes

\nabla_{x_{i}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\psi]=\mathrm{Cov}_{\pi_{i}}\!\big(\psi,\nabla_{x_{i}}s\big)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}\big[\psi\,\big(\nabla_{x_{i}}s-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{i}}[\nabla_{x_{i}}s]\big)\big].

Step 2. Linearization of log difference

For given continuously differentiable cost function
c:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, population potentials
f^{*}_{j}:\mathcal{X}_{j}\to\mathbb{R},\,j\in[m]
, we write for
i\in[m]
,

\displaystyle h_{x_{i}}(x_{-i})\;:=\;\exp\!\big((\oplus_{j\neq i}f^{*}_{j}(x_{j})-c(x_{i},x_{-i}))/\varepsilon\big),

and we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{i}}h_{x_{i}}(x_{-i})\;=\;-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,h_{x_{i}}(x_{-i})\,\nabla_{x_{i}}c(x_{i},x_{-i}).

We know the nondegeneracy condition through Proposition
2.2
that for some constant
b_{0}>0
,

V(x_{i})\;:=\;\nu_{-i}h_{x_{i}}\;=\;\int h_{x_{i}}(x_{-i})\,d\nu_{-i}(x_{-i})\geq\ b_{0}>0\quad\text{for all }x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}_{i}.

Lemma G.5
.

Define the linearized remainder

\Delta_{N}(x_{i})\;:=\;\log(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-i}h_{x_{i}})\;-\;\log(\nu_{-i}h_{x_{i}})\;-\;\frac{(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-i}-\nu_{-i})h_{x_{i}}}{\nu_{-i}h_{x_{i}}}.

Then under standard assumptions,

\sup_{x_{i}}|\Delta_{N}(x_{i})|\;=\;\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\!\Big(\frac{d\log N}{N}\Big)\;=\;o_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1/2}).

If, in addition,
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
, then

\sup_{x_{i}}\|\nabla_{x_{i}}\Delta_{N}(x_{i})\|=\sup_{x_{i}}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}\Delta_{N}(x_{i})\right|\;=\;\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\!\Big(\frac{d\log N}{N}\Big)\;=\;o_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1/2}).

Set

W(x_{i}):=(\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}-\nu_{-i})h_{x_{i}},\qquad V(x_{i}):=\nu_{-i}h_{x_{i}},\qquad u(x_{i}):=\frac{W(x_{i})}{V(x_{i})}.

Taylor expansion of
\log(1+u)
with integral remainder leads to

\Delta_{N}(x_{i})\;=\;-\tfrac{1}{2}\,u(x_{i})^{2}\;+\;R\big(u(x_{i})),

where

R(u)\;=\;\int_{0}^{u}\frac{(u-t)^{2}}{(1+t)^{3}}\,dt.

It is easily checked that

\displaystyle\qquad R(u)=\mathcal{O}(u^{3}),\quad R^{\prime}(u)=\mathcal{O}(u^{2})\quad\text{for}\ |u|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

Differentiation yields

\nabla_{x_{i}}\Delta_{N}(x_{i})\;=\;-\,u(x_{i})\,\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})\;+\;\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})R^{\prime}(u(x_{i})),

where

\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})\;=\;\frac{V(x_{i})\,\nabla_{x_{i}}W(x_{i})-W(x_{i})\,\nabla_{x_{i}}V(x_{i})}{V^{2}(x_{i})}\;=\;\frac{(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-i}-\nu_{-i})\nabla_{x_{i}}h_{x_{i}}}{V(x_{i})}\;-\;\frac{W(x_{i})}{V(x_{i})}\cdot\frac{\nu_{-i}\nabla_{x_{i}}h_{x_{i}}}{V(x_{i})}.

Differentiation under the integrals is justified by the regularity of

h_{x_{i}}
. Hence, for
h_{x_{i}}
and
\nabla_{x_{i}}h_{x_{i}}
bounded as a consequence of
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
,

W(x_{i})=(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-i}-\nu_{-i})h_{x_{i}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1/2}),\qquad(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-i}-\nu_{-i})\nabla_{x_{i}}h_{x_{i}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1/2}).

So
u(x_{i})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1/2})
and
\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1/2})
(pointwise in
x_{i}
). Therefore

u(x_{i})\,\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1}).

On the event
\sup_{x_{i}}|u(x_{i})|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
(which holds with probability
\to 1
),

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{i}}(R(u(x_{i}))\;=\;R^{\prime}(u(x_{i}))\,\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\!\big(|u(x_{i})|^{2}\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})\big)\;=\;o_{\mathbb{P}}\!\big(u(x_{i})\,\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})\big).

Hence
\nabla_{x_{i}}\Delta_{N}(x_{i})=-u(x_{i})\,\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(u(x_{i})\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i}))=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(N^{-1})
. If
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
in
x_{i}
with bounded
\nabla_{x_{i}}^{2}c
, then Lemma
G.6
gives

\sup_{x_{i}}|(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-i}-\nu_{-i})h_{x_{i}}|,\quad\sup_{x_{i}}\|(\hat{\nu}^{N}_{-i}-\nu_{-i})\nabla_{x_{i}}h_{x_{i}}\|\;=\;\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\!\Big(\sqrt{d\log N/N}\Big).

Thus
\sup_{x_{i}}|u(x_{i})|,\sup_{x_{i}}\|\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})\|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\!\big(\sqrt{d\log N/N}\big)
, thereby

\sup_{x_{i}}\|\nabla_{x_{i}}\Delta_{N}(x_{i})\|\;\leq\;\sup_{x_{i}}|u(x_{i})|\,\sup_{x_{i}}\|\nabla_{x_{i}}u(x_{i})\|(1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1))\;=\;\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\!\Big(d\log N/N\Big).

∎

The following lemma is presented in this section for its immediate use in the linearization of the EMOT system. However, it is of broad relevance and will be applied in subsequent developments in deriving weak limits.

Lemma G.6
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{X}_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is compact for
j\in[m]
and
K=(K_{1},\dots,K_{r}):\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
satisfies for some
i^{*}\in[m]
,

\sup_{y_{j}}\|K(y_{1},\dots,y_{(i^{*}-1)},x,y_{(i^{*}+1)},\dots,y_{m})-K(y_{1},\dots,y_{(i^{*}-1)},\tilde{x},y_{(i^{*}+1)},\dots,y_{m})\|_{\infty}\leq L\|x-\tilde{x}\|_{2},

and

\|K\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{\bm{y}\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j}}\|K(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{\bm{y}\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{X}_{j}}\max_{1\leq s\leq r}|K_{s}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})|<\infty.

Define

F_{N}(x)=\frac{1}{N^{m-1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\ell^{(i)}\leq N\\
1\leq i\leq m\\
i\neq i^{*}\end{subarray}}\overline{K}_{-i^{*}}(X_{\ell^{(1)}}^{(1)},\dots,X_{\ell^{(i^{*}-1)}}^{(i^{*}-1)},x,X_{\ell^{(i^{*}+1)}}^{(i^{*}+1)},\dots,X_{\ell^{(m)}}^{(m)}),

with
\overline{K}_{-i^{*}}(\cdot)
the centered version of
K(\cdot)
,

\displaystyle\overline{K}_{-i^{*}}(Z_{1},

\displaystyle\dots,Z_{(i^{*}-1)},x,Z_{(i^{*}+1)},\dots,Z_{m})

\displaystyle:=K(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{(i^{*}-1)},x,Z_{(i^{*}+1)},\dots,Z_{m})-\mathbb{E}K(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{(i^{*}-1)},x,Z_{(i^{*}+1)},\dots,Z_{m}).

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{i^{*}}}\|F_{N}(x)\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|K\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{d\log(L^{2}N)}{N}}.

For any fixed
x
, bounded difference inequality gives that for all
u>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|F_{N}(x)\|_{\infty}\geq u\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{C_{1}Nu^{2}}{\|K\|^{2}_{\infty}}\right)

for some universal constant
C_{1}>0
depending on
m,r,\|c\|_{\infty}
. For any
u>0
, pick a
\frac{u}{4L}
-net
\mathcal{N}_{\frac{u}{4L}}
of
\mathcal{X}_{i^{*}}
and then we have via the Lipschitzness,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{i^{*}}}\|F_{N}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{N}_{\frac{u}{4L}}}\|F_{N}(x)\|_{\infty}+\frac{u}{2}.

Note that
|\mathcal{N}_{\frac{u}{4L}}|\leq(\frac{C_{2}L}{u})^{d}
for some universal constant
C_{2}>0
depending on the diameter of
\mathcal{X}_{i^{*}}
. As a consequence, union bound provides

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{i^{*}}}\|F_{N}(x)\|_{\infty}\geq u\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{x\in\mathcal{N}_{\frac{u}{4L}}}\|F_{N}(x)\|_{\infty}\geq u/2\right)\leq\Big(\frac{C_{2}L}{u}\Big)^{d}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{1}Nu^{2}}{\|K\|^{2}_{\infty}}\right).

Let us define

v_{0}\ :=\ \max\!\Big\{1,\ \frac{d}{2}\,\log\left(\frac{L^{2}C_{1}C_{2}^{2}N}{\|K\|^{2}_{\infty}}\right)\Big\},\qquad u_{0}\ :=\ \|K\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{v_{0}}{C_{1}N}}.

Write
S:=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{i^{*}}}\|F_{N}(x)\|_{\infty}
and then split the tail integral at
u_{0}
:

\mathbb{E}S\ =\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(S>u)\,du\ \leq\ \underbrace{\int_{0}^{u_{0}}1\,du}_{=:I_{1}}\ +\ \underbrace{\int_{u_{0}}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{C_{2}L}{u}\Big)^{d}e^{-C_{1}Nu^{2}/\|K\|_{\infty}^{2}}\,du}_{=:I_{2}}.

Clearly
I_{1}=u_{0}
. For
I_{2}
, change of variables
v=C_{1}Nu^{2}/\|K\|^{2}_{\infty}
gives

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{2}^{d}L^{d}}{2}\,\frac{(C_{1}N)^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}}{\|K\|^{d-1}_{\infty}}\int_{v_{0}}^{\infty}v^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}e^{-v}\,dv

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}^{d}L^{d}}{2}\,\frac{(C_{1}N)^{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}}{\|K\|^{d-1}_{\infty}}\,v_{0}^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}\,e^{-v_{0}}.

By the definition of
v_{0}
,

v_{0}^{-(d+1)/2}\ \leq\ 1,\quad\quad e^{-v_{0}}\ \leq\ \left(\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}}{C_{2}L}\right)^{d}\Big(\frac{1}{C_{1}N}\Big)^{d/2}.

Substituting into the bound for
I_{2}
yields

I_{2}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\,(C_{1}N)^{-1/2}\|K\|_{\infty}.

Finally, using
v_{0}\leq\frac{d}{2}\log\left(\frac{L^{2}C_{1}C_{2}^{2}N}{\|K\|^{2}_{\infty}}\right)
when that term exceeds
1
(otherwise
v_{0}=1
and the displayed upper bound still holds), we obtain

\mathbb{E}S\ \ \leq\ \frac{\|K\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{C_{1}}}\,\sqrt{\frac{d\,\log\left(\frac{L^{2}C_{1}C_{2}^{2}N}{\|K\|^{2}_{\infty}}\right)}{2\,N}}+\frac{\|K\|_{\infty}}{2\sqrt{C_{1}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}.

∎

H
Technical details for operator norms estimates

This section collects several technical lemmas establishing bounds on the operator norms of auxiliary operators that appear throughout the paper. We focus on the operator norm bound of
\|\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{H}\|
through
\|\mathbb{D}-\mathbb{H}\|
and
\|\mathbb{D}-\mathbb{A}\|
. As in Appendix
F
and Appendix
G
, we follow the notation convention that

\mathcal{C}^{1}:=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{j})
.

First note that
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{H}
could be considered as operators on
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
due to multimarginal Schrödinger systems. Thus,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{H}\|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{1}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\leq\|\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\|\mathbb{A}-\mathbb{D}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}+\|\mathbb{D}-\mathbb{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}.

The bounds for the right-hand-side terms in the inequality are obtained in Lemma
H.1
and Lemma
H.2
, respectively.
∎

Lemma H.1
.

For any
i,j\in[m]
with
i\neq j
, we have that

\|\mathbb{D}-\mathbb{A}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left((m-1)\,\sqrt{\frac{d\log N}{N}}\right).

(94)

Lemma H.2
.

For any
i,j\in[m]
with
i\neq j
, we have that

\|\mathbb{D}-\mathbb{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left((m-1)^{2}\,N^{-1/(2\vee d)}(\log N)^{\mathbf{1}_{\{d=2\}}}\right).

(95)

By Lemma
H.4
, it suffices to show that

\|A_{ij}-D_{ij}\|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d\log N}{N}}\right).

(96)

We have that

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle(A_{ij}-D_{ij})f_{j}\|_{\infty}^{2}=\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\left|\int f_{j}(x_{j})(\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}-p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(\bm{x})d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}^{2}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\int\left|(\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}-p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(\bm{x})\right|^{2}d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{\|f_{j}\|^{2}_{\infty}}{N^{m-1}}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\sum_{\ell^{(1)},\dots,\ell^{(i-1)},\ell^{(i+1)},\dots,\ell^{(m)}}\left|(\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}-p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(X_{\ell^{(1)}}^{(1)},\dots,X_{\ell^{(i-1)}}^{(i-1)},x_{i},X_{\ell^{(i+1)}}^{(i+1)},\dots,X_{\ell^{(m)}}^{(m)})\right|^{2}.

This term is bounded by Lemma
D.7
. Using Proposition
D.2
, we have

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{i}}(A_{ij}-D_{ij})f_{j}\|_{\infty}=\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}\int f_{j}(x_{j})(\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}-p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(\bm{x})d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\left|\int f_{j}\left[\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x})\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[\hat{f}^{*}_{i}]-\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}c\right)-p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x})\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[f^{*}_{i}]-\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}c\right)\right]d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\int\left|(\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}-p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(\bm{x})\right|d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle+\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\left|\int\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x})\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[\hat{f}^{*}_{i}]-p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x})\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[f^{*}_{i}]d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})\right|

\displaystyle=\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\int\left|(\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}-p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(\bm{x})\right|d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle+\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\left|\int\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x})(\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[\hat{f}^{*}_{i}]-\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[f^{*}_{i}])+\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[f^{*}_{i}](\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x})-p^{*}_{\varepsilon}(\bm{x}))d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\int\left|(\hat{p}_{\varepsilon}-p^{*}_{\varepsilon})(\bm{x})\right|d\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}(\bm{x})+\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[\hat{f}^{*}_{i}]-\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}[f^{*}_{i}]\right|.

The proof is complete due to Lemma
D.7
and Lemma
4.8
.
∎

By Lemma
H.4
, it suffices to show that

\|D_{ij}-H_{ij}\|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left((m-1)N^{-1/(2\vee d)}(\log N)^{\mathbf{1}_{\{d=2\}}}\right).

(97)

Since
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
, direct differentiation of the Schrödinger system under the integral and Proposition
2.2
gives that
\|p_{\varepsilon}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}}\lesssim 1
. Denote

\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,0}}}p_{\varepsilon}^{*}:=p_{\varepsilon}^{*}
for notational convenience. Then, for

0\leq k\leq d
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}\bigl((D_{ij}-H_{ij})f_{j}\bigr)(x_{i})=\int f_{j}(x_{j})\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}p_{\varepsilon}^{*}(x_{i},x_{-i})\,d(\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}-\nu_{-i})(x_{-i}),

where
k=0
denotes the function itself. Moreover, by the product
rule, for
\nabla_{x_{-i}}:=(\nabla_{x_{\ell}})_{\ell\neq i}

\max_{0\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\Bigl\|\nabla_{x_{-i}}\bigl(f_{j}(x_{j})\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}p_{\varepsilon}^{*}(x_{i},\cdot)\bigr)\Bigr\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}.

Therefore, Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality

[
91
]
gives

\displaystyle\|(D_{ij}-H_{ij})f_{j}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{0\leq k\leq d}\sup_{x_{i}\in[-1,1]^{d}}\left|\int f_{j}(x_{j})\frac{\partial}{\partial_{x_{i,k}}}p_{\varepsilon}^{*}(x_{i},x_{-i})\,d(\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N}-\nu_{-i})(x_{-i})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}W_{1}(\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N},\nu_{-i}).

Taking the supremum over
\|f_{j}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq 1
, we obtain

\|D_{ij}-H_{ij}\|\lesssim W_{1}(\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N},\nu_{-i}).

Taking the product of optimal couplings between

\hat{\nu}_{k}^{N}
and
\nu_{k}
,
k\neq i
, gives

W_{1}(\hat{\nu}_{-i}^{N},\nu_{-i})\leq\sum_{k\neq i}W_{1}(\hat{\nu}_{k}^{N},\nu_{k}).

Standard empirical Wasserstein bounds yield that
[
63
,
91
,
93
]
,

W_{1}(\hat{\nu}_{k}^{N},\nu_{k})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(N^{-1/(2\vee d)}(\log N)^{\mathbf{1}_{\{d=2\}}}\right),\qquad k\in[m]

and the proof is complete.

∎

Lemma
F.1
gives

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}\left([\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\right)-\varepsilon\mathfrak{L}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{d\log N}{N}\right).

(98)

Lemma
F.2
and Lemma
H.1
gives that

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}\left([\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\right)-\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}\left([\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}\left(N^{-1/2}\right).

(99)

Lemma
F.2
and Lemma
H.2
gives that on some event
E^{(2)}_{N}
with
\mathbb{P}(E^{(2)}_{N})\to 1
as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\left\|\bm{\Gamma}\left([\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\right)-\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}\left([\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}\left(N^{-1/2}\right).

(100)

As a consequence of (
98
), (
99
) and (
100
),

\displaystyle\left\|\bm{\Gamma}\left([\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\right)-\varepsilon\mathfrak{L}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}\left(N^{-1/2}\right).

As mentioned in Step 4,
\bm{\Gamma}
is invertible and
\|\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}\|\leq C
for some constant. Thus,

\displaystyle\left\|\left([\bm{f}^{*}]-[\hat{\bm{f}}^{*}]\right)-\varepsilon\bm{\Gamma}^{-1}\mathfrak{L}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}\left(N^{-1/2}\right).

∎

Some useful properties on linear operator theory are listed below.

Lemma H.3
.

Given an operator
S:\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}
defined as

S(\bm{f}):=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1}\bm{f}\\
\vdots\\
L_{m}\bm{f}\\
\end{pmatrix}

with
L_{i}:\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1]^{d})
bounded operator defined for
i\in[m]
with
\|L_{i}\|\leq M
, we have

\|S\|\leq M.

We see that

\|Sf\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\|(Sf)_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\|L_{i}\|\|\bm{f}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq M\|\bm{f}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}.

∎

Lemma H.4
.

Given an operator
S:\mathcal{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{C}^{1}
defined as

S\begin{pmatrix}f_{1}\\
\vdots\\
f_{m}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{j\neq 1}S_{1j}f_{j}\\
\vdots\\
\sum_{j\neq m}S_{mj}f_{j}\\
\end{pmatrix}

with
S_{ij}:\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1]^{d})\to\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1]^{d})
bounded operator defined for
i\neq j
with
\|S_{ij}\|\leq K
, we have

\|S\|\leq(m-1)K.

We see that

\|Sf\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\|(Sf)_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\sum_{j\neq i}\|S_{ij}\|\|f_{j}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq(m-1)K\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}.

∎

I
Omitted proofs in the main paper

Suppose
\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon}
(resp.
\tilde{\pi}^{*}_{\varepsilon}
) is the optimal coupling of the
m
-marginal Schrödinger system
S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
(resp.
S_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\nu}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\nu}_{m})
) and

\gamma\in\Pi(\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\pi}^{*}_{\varepsilon})
is the optimal coupling attaining
W_{1}(\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\pi}^{*}_{\varepsilon})
.
Define
\pi_{0}\in\Pi\left({T_{\alpha}}_{\sharp}\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon},{T_{\alpha}}_{\sharp}\tilde{\pi}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\right)
via

\pi_{0}=(T_{\alpha},T_{\alpha})_{\sharp}\gamma,

namely,
\pi_{0}(A\times B)=\gamma(T_{\alpha}^{-1}(A)\times T_{\alpha}^{-1}(B))
for
A,\,B
measurable. Notice that
T_{\alpha}(\bm{x})
is continuous, and we have

\displaystyle W_{1}(\bar{\nu}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon})=W_{1}({T_{\alpha}}_{\sharp}\pi^{*}_{\varepsilon},{T_{\alpha}}_{\sharp}\tilde{\pi}^{*}_{\varepsilon})\leq\int\|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\|d\pi_{0}(\bm{x},\bm{y})

\displaystyle=\int\|T_{\alpha}(\bm{x})-T_{\alpha}(\bm{y})\|d\gamma(\bm{x},\bm{y})\leq\int\|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\|d\gamma(\bm{x},\bm{y})=W_{1}(\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*},\tilde{\pi}_{\varepsilon}),

using the Lipschiz property of
T_{\alpha}(\bm{x})
. Moreover, Theorem 3.3 in
[
9
]
implies that under this setting,

W_{1}(\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*},\tilde{\pi}_{\varepsilon})\leq\sqrt{m}W_{2}(\bm{\nu},\bm{\tilde{\nu}})+CW_{2}(\bm{\nu},\bm{\tilde{\nu}})^{1/2}.

(102)

Here,
C>0
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon
, and the second moment of
\nu_{j}
and
\tilde{\nu}_{j}
for
j\in[m]
. Combining (
I
) and (
102
), the proof is complete.
∎

The multimarginal Schrödinger system (
7
) yields

\displaystyle e^{-\|c\|_{\infty}/\varepsilon}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{-j}}\exp\!\left(\frac{\sum_{i\neq j}f_{i}^{*}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}\right)\,d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{X}_{-j}}\exp\!\left(\frac{\sum_{i\neq j}f_{i}^{*}(x_{i})-c(x_{j},x_{-j})}{\varepsilon}\right)\,d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})

\displaystyle=e^{-f_{j}^{*}(x_{j})/\varepsilon}\leq e^{\|c\|_{\infty}/\varepsilon}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{-j}}\exp\!\left(\frac{\sum_{i\neq j}f_{i}^{*}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}\right)\,d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})

for
\nu_{j}
-almost every
x_{j}
. Namely,

-\|c\|_{\infty}\leq f_{j}^{*}(x_{j})+\varepsilon\log\int_{\mathcal{X}_{-j}}\exp\!\left(\frac{\sum_{i\neq j}f_{i}^{*}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}\right)\,d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})\leq\|c\|_{\infty}.

As a result,

\|f_{j}^{*}-\int f_{j}^{*}\,d\nu_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\nu_{j})}\leq 2\|c\|_{\infty}.

(103)

Nonnegativity of relative entropy therefore gives

S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m})\geq-\|c\|_{\infty}.

Conversely, the product measure
\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k}
is an admissible coupling, so

S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m})\leq\int c\,d(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})+\varepsilon\operatorname{KL}(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k}\|\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})=\int c\,d(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})\leq\|c\|_{\infty}.

Also note that the multimarginal Schrödinger system (
7
) and the normalization of the optimal potential gives that

\displaystyle S_{\varepsilon}(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int f_{j}^{*}\,d\nu_{j}=\int f_{m}^{*}\,d\nu_{m}.

(104)

Finally,

\displaystyle\biggl|\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}^{*}(x_{j})\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl|\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int f_{j}^{*}\,d\nu_{j}\biggr|+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\biggl|f_{j}^{*}(x_{j})-\int f_{j}^{*}\,d\nu_{j}\biggr|\leq(2m+1)\|c\|_{\infty}.

This completes the proof.
∎

By the duality of operator norm, we can write

\|\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f})\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}=\sup\left\{\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}),\mathbf{g}\right\rangle_{\mathscr{L}_{m}},\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}\leq 1\right\}.

(105)

Note that

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{f}),\mathbf{g}\rangle_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int\left[g_{j}\left(1-\exp\left({\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}-c}{\varepsilon}}\right)\right)\right]d\left(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\int g_{j}^{2}d\nu_{j}\right)^{1/2}\left\{\int\left[\int 1-\exp\left({\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}-c}{\varepsilon}}\right)d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})\right]^{2}d\nu_{j}\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int g_{j}^{2}d\nu_{j}\right)^{1/2}\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int\left[\int 1-\exp\left({\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}-c}{\varepsilon}}\right)d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})\right]^{2}d\nu_{j}\right\}^{1/2},

where the last two inequalities both follow from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, with the equality attained if
g_{j}=\int\left(1-\exp\left({\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}-c}{\varepsilon}}\right)\right)d\nu_{-j}(x_{-j})
,
for
j\in[m]
. This completes the proof.
∎

For
\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m}),\,\mathbf{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\in\mathcal{S}_{L}
,
t\in[0,1]
, we define

\displaystyle h(t):=\Phi_{\varepsilon}((1-t)\mathbf{f}+t\mathbf{g})

\displaystyle=(1-t)\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int f_{j}d\nu_{j}+t\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int g_{j}d\nu_{j}-\varepsilon\int\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left((1-t)f_{j}+tg_{j}\right)-c}{\varepsilon}\right)d\left(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j}\right)+\varepsilon.

Taking derivatives, we get

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int(g_{j}-f_{j})d\nu_{j}-\int\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}(g_{j}-f_{j})\right)\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left((1-t)f_{j}+tg_{j}\right)-c}{\varepsilon}\right)d\left(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}\left((1-t)\mathbf{f}+t\mathbf{g}\right),\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{f}\right\rangle_{\mathscr{L}_{m}},

\displaystyle h^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}(g_{j}-f_{j})\right)^{2}\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left((1-t)f_{j}+tg_{j}\right)-c}{\varepsilon}\right)d\left(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j}\right).

The strong concavity (
11
) could be rewritten as

h(0)-h(1)\geq-h^{\prime}(0)+\frac{\beta}{2}\|\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}^{2}.

(106)

It suffices to show that
h^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(t)\leq-\beta\|\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathscr{L}_{m}}^{2},
for all
t\in[0,1]
, namely

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}(g_{j}-f_{j})\right)^{2}\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left((1-t)f_{j}+tg_{j}\right)-c}{\varepsilon}\right)d\left(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j}\right)\geq\beta\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int\left(g_{j}-f_{j}\right)^{2}d\nu_{j}

\displaystyle=\beta\int\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(g_{j}-f_{j}\right)^{2}d(\otimes_{j=1}\nu_{k})=\beta\int\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}(g_{j}-f_{j})\right)^{2}d(\otimes_{j=1}\nu_{k}),

(107)

where we make use of the fact that
\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{S}_{L}
to derive the last equality.
The fact that
\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{S}_{L}
indicates that
\beta=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{L+\|c\|_{\infty}}{\varepsilon}\right)
qualifies to make (
107
) hold true.
∎

J
Technical lemmas

Lemma J.1
(Tightness of couplings).

Let
\mathcal{Y}=\Pi_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{Y}_{j}
. Assume that
\mathscr{{T}}_{j}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}_{j})
is tight for
j\in[m]
. Then the set
\Pi(\mathscr{T}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{T}_{m}):=\{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\mid{e_{j}}_{\sharp}\gamma\in\mathscr{T}_{j}\}

is tight. Particularly, we have the tightness of
\Pi(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})=\{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\mid{e_{j}}_{\sharp}\gamma=\nu_{j}\}
.

Let
\delta>0
. By the tightness of
\mathscr{T}_{j}
we can find a compact set
K_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{j}
such that
\mu_{j}(\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus K_{j})<\frac{\delta}{m}
, for any
\mu_{j}\in\mathscr{T}_{j}
.

Let
K:=K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{m}
and let
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathscr{T}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{T}_{m})
. Due to the fact
{e_{j}}_{\sharp}\gamma\in\mathscr{T}_{j}
, and the fact that

\mathcal{Y}\setminus K\subseteq\left(\left((\mathcal{Y}_{1}\setminus K_{1})\times\prod_{k=2}^{m}\mathcal{Y}_{k}\right)\bigcup\left(\mathcal{Y}_{1}\times(\mathcal{Y}_{2}\setminus K_{2})\times\prod_{k=3}^{m}\mathcal{Y}_{k}\right)\bigcup\dots\bigcup\left(\prod_{k=1}^{m-1}\mathcal{Y}_{k}\times(\mathcal{Y}_{m}\setminus K_{m})\right)\right),

one has
\gamma(\mathcal{Y}\setminus K)\leq\delta
.
∎

Lemma J.2
(Polyak-Łojasiewicz inequality).

Let
S\subset\mathbb{H}
be a convex subset of a Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
and
f:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
\beta
-strongly convex function on
S
. Then, we have for all
x\in S
,

f(x)-\inf_{y\in\mathbb{H}}f(y)\leq\frac{1}{2\beta}\|\nabla f(x)\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}.

See
[
56
]
.
∎

Lemma J.3
(Hoeffding’s inequality in Hilbert space).

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent mean-zero random variables taking values in a Hilbert space
(\mathbb{H},\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{H}})
. If
\|X_{i}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq C
for some constant
C>0
, then for every
t>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}}\geq t\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-{\frac{t^{2}}{8nC^{2}}}\right).

(108)

See Lemma 17 in
[
79
]
.
∎

Lemma J.4
(Dudley’s entropy integral bound).

Let
\{X_{t},\,t\in T\}
be a zero-mean process satisfying the sub-Gaussian condition with respect to distance
\rho_{X}
, i.e., for any
t,\tilde{t}\in T
,
\mathbb{P}(|X_{t}-X_{\tilde{t}}|\geq\tau)\leq C_{1}\exp\left({-\frac{C_{2}\tau^{2}}{\rho^{2}_{X}(t,\tilde{t})}}\right)
for some universal constant
C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Then for any
\delta\in[0,D]
, with
D=\sup_{t,\tilde{t}\in T}\rho_{X}(t,\tilde{t})
denoting the diameter of
T
under
\rho_{X}
, we have, for some universal constants
C_{3},C_{4}>0
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t,\tilde{t}\in T}(X_{t}-X_{\tilde{t}})\right]\leq C_{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in T\\
\rho_{X}(\gamma,\gamma^{\prime})\leq\delta\end{subarray}}(X_{\gamma}-X_{\gamma^{\prime}})\right]+C_{4}\int_{\delta/4}^{D}\sqrt{\log N(T,\varepsilon,\rho_{X})}d\varepsilon.

See Theorem 5.22 in
[
92
]
.
∎

Once the order of the difference of the empirical and population potential is settled as in Lemma
4.8
,
the following lemma is a straightforward extension of Lemma B.1 in
[
47
]
from two marginal case to non-smooth multimarginal case. The proof is just Taylor expansion of exponential function as suggested by
[
47
]
.

Lemma J.5
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a compact set,
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
, and let
\hat{\nu}^{N}_{1},\dots,\hat{\nu}^{N}_{m}
be their empirical measures. Then

\displaystyle\left\|{e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})-f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}\right)}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\frac{\log N}{N}\right),

(109)

\displaystyle\left\|{e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log N}{N}}\right).

(110)

Since
c\in C^{2}
, Proposition
2.2
, Proposition
D.2
, and the Schrödinger equations imply that the population and empirical potentials are uniformly bounded in
C^{1}
. Thus we have, via second-order Taylor expansion

\displaystyle\left\|{e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})-\bar{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}\right)}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\left\|{e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bar{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})-\bar{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}\right)}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})-\bar{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\right).

By virtue of Lemma
4.8
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})-\bar{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\sqrt{\log N/N}\bigr),

(111)

and then (
109
) follows. To prove (
110
), apply the reverse triangle inequality to (
109
):

\displaystyle\left\|e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})-\bar{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}

(112)

\displaystyle\geq\left\|e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}-\left\|e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})-\bar{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}.

Apply (
111
) , (
112
) and Proposition
2.2
to get:

\left\|e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\left\|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})-\bar{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}\left(N^{-1/2}\right)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\sqrt{\log N/N}\bigr).

∎

Lemma J.6
(Vanishing term B).

B=\sqrt{N}\int g(\bm{x})e^{-\frac{c(\bm{x})}{\varepsilon}}(e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}})d\left(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\hat{\nu}^{N}_{k}-\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k}\right)(\bm{x})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Set
\mathcal{H}:=\{fge^{-c/\varepsilon},f\in\mathcal{F}\}
with
\mathcal{F}=\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1]^{md}),\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\leq 1\}
. For any
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
, note that
\|fge^{-c/\varepsilon}-f^{\prime}ge^{-c/\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\leq\|ge^{-c/\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\,\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
, hence any
(\varepsilon/\|ge^{-c/\varepsilon}\|_{\infty})
-net
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{K}\}
of
\mathcal{F}
gives an

\varepsilon
-net
\{f_{1}ge^{-c/\varepsilon},\ldots,f_{K}ge^{-c/\varepsilon}\}
of
\mathcal{H}
. Therefore
\sup_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\|h\|_{\infty}\leq\|ge^{-c/\varepsilon}\|_{\infty}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\|f\|_{\infty}<\infty
and

\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{H},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon/\|ge^{-c/\varepsilon}\|_{\infty},\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}).

The remaining arguments follows the chaining argument Lemma
J.4
, same as that in the proof of Theorem
5.5
in Section
D
by virtue of the sub-Gaussianity from Lemma
D.1
, now with dimension
md
. Namely, we have, up to some constant depending on
m,\|g\|_{\infty},\varepsilon,d
,

\displaystyle|B|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(N^{\,1/2-1/(2\vee md)}(\log N)^{\mathbf{1}_{\{md=2\}}}\Bigl\|e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}\hat{f}^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}-e^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{*}_{i}(x_{i})}{\varepsilon}}\Bigr\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}\biggr).

Combining with Lemma
J.5
and the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma J.7
.

Suppose
\Phi(x_{\alpha}^{(j)};\alpha=i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}},j=1,\dots,m)
is a bounded kernel of order
m
and degree
(m_{1},\dots,m_{m})
with
m_{k}>1
for some
k\in[m]
, symmetric inside each group. Then given
m
independent series
\{X^{(j)}_{1},\dots,X^{(j)}_{N}\},j=1,\dots,m
of independent random variables, for

\displaystyle V_{N}:=\frac{1}{N^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{jk}\leq N\\
1\leq k\leq m_{j}\\
j=1,\dots,m\end{subarray}}\Phi(X_{\alpha}^{(j)};\alpha=i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}},j=1,\dots,m),

and

\displaystyle U_{N}:=\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m}\tbinom{N}{m_{k}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{j1}<\dots<i_{jm_{j}}\leq N\\
\,j=1,\dots,m\end{subarray}}\Phi(X_{\alpha}^{(j)};\alpha=i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}},j=1,\dots,m),

we have

\displaystyle|V_{N}-U_{N}|\lesssim\frac{\|\Phi\|_{\infty}}{N}.

Due to the symmetry of the kernel
\Phi
inside each group, we could write

\displaystyle U_{N}=\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m}(N)_{m_{k}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}}\leq N\\
i_{jp}\neq i_{jq},\,1\leq p<q\leq m_{j}\\
j=1,\dots,m\end{subarray}}\Phi(X_{\alpha}^{(j)};\alpha=i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}},j=1,\dots,m),

where
(N)_{m_{k}}=N(N-1)\dots(N-m_{k}+1)
denotes the falling factorial notation. At the same time, observe that

\displaystyle V_{N}-U_{N}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{N^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}}-\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m}(N)_{m_{k}}}\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}}\leq N\\
i_{jp}\neq i_{jq},\,1\leq p<q\leq m_{j}\\
j=1,\dots,m\end{subarray}}\Phi(X_{\alpha}^{(j)};\alpha=i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}},j=1,\dots,m)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}}\sum_{*}\Phi(X_{\alpha}^{(j)};\alpha=i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}},j=1,\dots,m)

\displaystyle:=A+B,

where
\sum_{*}
denotes the summation over all index
(i_{j\alpha};\alpha=i_{j_{1}},\dots,i_{j_{m_{j}}},j=1,\dots,m)
,
1\leq i_{j1},\dots,i_{jm_{j}}\leq N,j=1,\dots,m
with
i_{jp}=i_{jq}
for some
1\leq p<q\leq m_{j}
.
We have, by the boundedness of the kernel, say,
|\Phi(\cdot)|\leq C
, for term A,

\displaystyle|A|

\displaystyle\leq C\prod_{k=1}^{m}(N)_{m_{k}}\left|\frac{1}{N^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}}-\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m}(N)_{m_{k}}}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{N^{-m+\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}}\left|\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}-1}(N-j)-N^{-m+\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{N}.

Here the last inequality follows from the fact that the highest degree term of the polynomial
\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}-1}(N-j)
equals
N^{-m+\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}
and the assumption that
m_{k}>1
for some
k\in[m]
.
Similarly, for term B, noticing that there are
N^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}-\prod_{k=1}^{m}(N)_{m_{k}}
many terms in the summation
\sum_{*}
,

\displaystyle|B|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}}\left|N^{\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}-\prod_{k=1}^{m}(N)_{m_{k}}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{N^{-m+\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}}\left|N^{-m+\sum_{k=1}^{m}m_{k}}-\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}-1}(N-j)\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{N},

following the same reasoning as above.
∎

K
Other Auxiliary tools

We briefly recapitulate useful tools related to bootstrap consistency in this section, in particular, Hadamard differentiability and functional delta method. For two normed spaces
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{C}
, a map
\phi:\Theta\subset\mathfrak{D}\to\mathfrak{C}
is called Hadamard directionally differentiable at
\theta\in\Theta
if there exists a map
\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}:\mathfrak{F}_{\Theta}(\theta)\to\mathfrak{C}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{\phi(\theta_{t})-\phi(\theta)}{t}=\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}(h),

(113)

for any sequence
(\theta_{t})_{t>0}\subset\Theta
with
\frac{\theta_{t}-\theta}{t}\to h
as
t\to 0^{+}
.
Here,
\mathfrak{F}_{\Theta}(\theta)
is the tangent cone to
\Theta
at
\theta
defined as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\Theta}(\theta)=\{h\in\mathfrak{D},\quad h=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta_{t}\to\theta\in\Theta\\
t\to 0^{+}\end{subarray}}\frac{\theta_{t}-\theta}{t}\}.

The derivative
\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}
can be shown to be continuous and positively homogeneous but not necessarily linear. If (
113
) holds for
h\in\mathfrak{D}_{0}
for some subset
\mathfrak{D}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\Theta}(\theta)
, then we say
\phi
is Hadamard directionally differentiable at
\theta\in\Theta
tangentially to
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
. In such case, the derivative
\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}
is only valid on
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
. Finally, if the derivative
\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}
is linear, we call
\phi
to be Hadamard differentiable at
\theta
(tangentially to
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
if
\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}
is defined only on
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
).

Lemma K.1
(Chain rule for Hadamard differentiability).

If
\phi:\Theta_{\phi}\subset\mathfrak{D}\to\mathfrak{C}
is Hadamard differentiable at
\theta\in\Theta_{\phi}
tangentially to
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
and
\psi:\Theta_{\psi}\subset\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{L}
is Hadamard differentiable at
\phi(\theta)
tangentially to
\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}(\mathfrak{D}_{0})
, then
\psi\circ\phi:\Theta_{\phi}\to\mathfrak{L}
is Hadamard differentiable at
\theta
tangentially to
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
with derivative
\psi^{\prime}_{\phi(\theta)}\circ\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}
.

Lemma K.2
(Functional delta method).

Let
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{C}
be two normed spaces, and a map
\phi:\Theta\subset\mathfrak{D}\to\mathfrak{C}
that is Hadamard directionally differentiable at
\theta\in\Theta
tangentially to some set
\mathfrak{D}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{\Theta}(\theta)
. Let
T_{n}:\Omega\to\Theta
be maps such that
r_{n}(T_{n}-\theta)\overset{w}{\longrightarrow}T

for some
r_{n}\to\infty
and Borel measurable map
T:\Omega\to\mathfrak{D}
with values in a separable subset of
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
. Then

•

r_{n}(\phi(T_{n})-\phi(\theta))\overset{w}{\longrightarrow}\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}(T)
;

•

If
\Theta
is also convex and
\mathfrak{D}_{0}=\mathfrak{F}_{\Theta}(\theta)
, then
r_{n}\left(\phi(T_{n})-\phi(\theta)-\phi^{\prime}_{\theta}(T_{n}-\theta)\right)\to 0
in outer probability.

For more details on outer probability, see
[
89
]
. The proofs of the previous two theorems could be found in
[
89
,
80
]
.

The proof of either Theorem
4.9
or Theorem
4.10
is the same, basically applying Theorem 23.9 in
[
88
]
. Let us first verify the Hadamard differentiability. Recall that the following map
\mathcal{H}
is Hadamard differentiable by Theorem 6 in
[
44
]
. It is assumed that
c\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
in
[
44
]
for simplicity and essentially one only needs
c\in\mathcal{C}^{2k}
with
k\geq d/2+1
by tracking their proof.

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}:

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{j})\quad\to\quad\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathcal{X}_{j})

\displaystyle\bm{\nu}:=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})\mapsto\mathcal{H}(\bm{\nu})=(f^{*}_{1},\dots,f^{*}_{m})

with
(f^{*}_{1},\dots,f^{*}_{m})
denotes the Schrödinger potential associated to
\bm{\nu}:=(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})
.
As a consequence,

S_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\nu})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j}(f^{*}_{j})

and

F(\bm{\nu}):=\int g(\bm{x})\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}f^{*}_{j}(x_{j})-c(\bm{x})}{\varepsilon}\right)d\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\nu_{j}(x_{j})

are both Hadamard differentiable. This fact comes obvious once we realize

S_{\varepsilon}(\bm{\nu})=H_{1}(\bm{\nu},\mathcal{H}(\bm{\nu}))

with

\displaystyle H_{1}:\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{j})\times\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathcal{X}_{j})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(\bm{\nu},\bm{f})

\displaystyle\mapsto H_{1}(\bm{\nu},\bm{f})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\int f_{j}(x_{j})d\nu_{j}(x_{j})

being Hadamard differentiable and

F(\bm{\nu})=H_{2}\left((\text{id},\mathcal{H})(\bm{\nu})\right)

with

\displaystyle H_{2}:\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{j})\times\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathcal{X}_{j})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(\bm{\nu},\bm{f})

\displaystyle\mapsto H_{2}(\bm{\nu},\bm{f})=\int g(\bm{x})\exp\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}(x_{j})-c(\bm{x})}{\varepsilon}\right)d(\otimes_{k=1}^{m}\nu_{k})(\bm{x})

Hadamard differentiable. Define
\mathcal{F}_{0,j}:=\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathcal{X}_{j}),\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}}\leq R_{k}\}
for some constant
R_{k}>0
depending on
c,\varepsilon,d
and we know that the optimal potential
f^{*}_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{0,j}
. For
f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{0,j}
, to shorten the notation, we denote
\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}(x_{j})
as
f_{1}\oplus f_{2}\oplus\dots\oplus f_{m}
. After that, define

\mathcal{F}^{*}:=\left\{f_{1}\oplus f_{2}\oplus\dots\oplus f_{m};f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{0,j},j\in[m]\right\},

we need to check that as maps into
\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{*})
, the sequence

\sqrt{N}\left(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\nu}_{j}^{B}-\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\nu}^{N}_{j}\right)

is asymptotically measurable and converges conditionally in distribution to

\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}G^{(j)}_{\nu_{j}}(f_{j})\right)_{\oplus_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}

given
(X^{(1)}_{1},\dots,X^{(m)}_{1}),(X^{(1)}_{2},\dots,X^{(m)}_{2}),\dots,(X^{(1)}_{N},\dots,X^{(m)}_{N})
. Here,
G^{(j)}_{\nu_{j}}
are independent and the weak limits of
\sqrt{N}\left(\hat{\nu}_{j}^{N}-\nu_{j}\right)
,
j\in[m]
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{0,j}
is Donsker w.r.t.
\nu_{j}
(cf.
[
89
]
), each of

\sqrt{N}\bigl(\hat{\nu}_{j}^{B}-\hat{\nu}^{N}_{j}\bigr)

is asymptotically measurable and converges conditionally in distribution for
j\in[m]

(cf. Chapter 3.6 in
[
89
]
).
By Lemma 1.4.4 and Example 1.4.6 in
[
89
]
, as maps into

\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{0,1})\times\dots\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{0,m})
, the sequence

\left(\sqrt{N}(\hat{\nu}_{1}^{B}-\hat{\nu}_{1}^{N}),\dots,\sqrt{N}(\hat{\nu}_{m}^{B}-\hat{\nu}_{m}^{N})\right)

is asymptotically measurable and converges conditionally in distribution to

(G^{(1)}_{\nu_{1}},\dots,G^{(m)}_{\nu_{m}})
. Since the map

\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{0,1})\times\dots\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{0,m})\ni(q_{1},\dots,q_{m})\mapsto\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}q_{j}(f_{j})\right)_{\oplus_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{*})

is continuous, we see that, as maps into
\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{*})
,

\sqrt{N}\left(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\nu}_{j}^{B}-\otimes_{j=1}^{m}\hat{\nu}^{N}_{j}\right)

is asymptotically measurable and converges conditionally in distribution to

\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}G^{(j)}_{\nu_{j}}(f_{j})\right)_{\oplus_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}^{*}}

as desired. The rest follows from Theorem 23.9 in
[
88
]
.
∎

L
Bootstrap verification

For each specified triple of marginal distributions and sample
size
n\in\{50,100,250,500,1000\}
, we generate
R=5000
independent
datasets, each consisting of
n
independent observations from each
of the three marginal distributions. The three marginal samples
are independent. For dataset
r
, let

\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(r)}
denote the empirical plug-in estimate of a scalar target

\theta^{*}
, obtained by solving the empirical entropic transport
problem. Independently within each marginal sample, we draw

B=4999
bootstrap resamples of size
n
with replacement.
We refit the transport problem for each resample, obtaining

\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(r,b)}
,
b=1,\ldots,B
. In the experiment, we consider

\theta^{*}\in\Bigl\{S_{\varepsilon},\int(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}}{3})^{2}d\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*},F(t):=\int\mathbf{1}_{\{c(x,y,z)\leq t\}}d\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}\Bigr\}

where

S_{\varepsilon}=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})}\left\{\int c\,d\pi+\varepsilon\operatorname{KL}\!\left(\pi\,\middle\|\,\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}\otimes\mu_{3}\right)\right\}

is the cost functional and

\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}
denotes the corresponding optimal coupling.
\int(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}}{3})^{2}d\pi_{\varepsilon}^{*}
and
F(t)

assess bootstrap validity for the Schrödinger coupling integrated against specific test functions.

F(t)
is called colocalization curve
[
48
]
.

We consider both regularization parameters

\varepsilon\in\{0.05,0.2\}
and both squared and unsquared cost functions

c_{1}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq 3}|x_{j}-x_{k}|,\qquad c_{2}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq 3}(x_{j}-x_{k})^{2}.

For every specified marginal triple, we include all combinations

(c,\varepsilon,n)\in\{c_{1},c_{2}\}\times\{0.05,0.2\}\times\{50,100,250,500,1000\}.

Thus, there are
20
conditions per marginal triple.
Define the centered bootstrap differences

D_{n}^{(r,b)}=|\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(r,b)}-\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(r)}|,

and let
Q_{r,u}^{B}
denote their empirical
u
-quantile.
At nominal confidence level
\gamma
, the basic bootstrap
interval is

I_{n,\gamma}^{(r)}=\left[\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(r)}-Q_{r,\gamma}^{B},\;\hat{\theta}_{n}^{(r)}+Q_{r,\gamma}^{B}\right].

Its empirical coverage is

\widehat{C}_{n}(\gamma)=\frac{1}{R}\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathbf{1}\left\{\theta^{*}\in I_{n,\gamma}^{(r)}\right\},

where we approximate
\theta^{*}
by solving the population entropic transport problem using a refined quadrature discretization of the known marginal distributions, independently of the simulated datasets, since closed-form population values are unavailable.

Figure
S1
summarizes bootstrap
calibration for the four groups of marginal configurations considered below.
For each statistic and each combination of marginal triples,
cost, regularization parameter, and sample size, we report

100\max_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\bigl|\widehat{C}_{n}(\gamma)-\gamma\bigr|
,
with

\Gamma=\{0.05,0.10,\ldots,0.95\}.

Each entry therefore represents
the largest absolute calibration error over the specified
confidence levels, expressed in percentage points.

The corresponding calibration curves are presented in
Figures
S2
,

S3
,

S4
, and

S5
. Each curve plots empirical coverage against
nominal confidence for a fixed sample size. The diagonal
y=x

indicates exact calibration.

Fig S1:

The input observations are
X_{j}=2Y_{j}-1
, with samples drawn
independently from the three marginal distributions.
Each entry is

100\max_{\gamma\in\Gamma}|\widehat{C}_{n}(\gamma)-\gamma|
,
where
\Gamma=\{0.05,0.10,\ldots,0.95\}
and

\widehat{C}_{n}(\gamma)
denotes empirical coverage at nominal
confidence level
\gamma
.
Entries are expressed in percentage points and rounded
to one decimal place.

c_{1}
,
\varepsilon=0.05

(b) Unsquared Euclidean cost:
c_{1}
,
\varepsilon=0.2

c_{2}
,
\varepsilon=0.05

c_{2}
,
\varepsilon=0.2
